The opposite in this definition keeps our left-module convention. The endomorphism-algebra connection with dominant dimension is classical, going back to Mueller (Mueller 1968, Lemma 3 and Theorem 4) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Mueller1968](#ref-Mueller1968)]; the precise Tachikawa–Nakayama correspondence for a pair $(A,M)$ is also stated in (Chen and Xi 2025, 7288) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[ChenXi2025TAMS](#ref-ChenXi2025TAMS)]. Section <a href="#sec:consequences" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:consequences">8</a> uses this connection and constructs the modules $S$ and $W$ directly. Taking $K$ to be an algebraic closure of $k$ gives examples over an algebraically closed field of characteristic two. This field contains the displayed transcendental parameters; the assertion does not concern the algebraic closure of a finite field.

The same resolution also gives a finitistic-dimension consequence. For a finite-dimensional algebra, the *little left finitistic dimension* is the supremum of the finite projective dimensions of finite left modules; the *big left finitistic dimension* allows all left modules instead.

<a id="stmt-cor-finitistic"></a>
<div id="cor:finitistic" class="corollary">

**Corollary 3** (Finitistic dimensions). *Let $k$ be the field in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, let $K/k$ be any field extension, and let $\Gamma_K$ be the algebra in Corollary <a href="#cor:homological" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:homological">2</a>. For every integer $n\geq1$, there is a finite-dimensional left $\Gamma_K$-module $C_n$ such that $$\mathop{\mathrm{pd}}_{\Gamma_K}C_n=n.$$ Consequently $\Gamma_K$ has infinite little left finitistic dimension and infinite big left finitistic dimension.*

</div>

This gives an independent characteristic-two example alongside the complex algebra of (OpenAI 2026a, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[OpenAIFinitistic2026](#ref-OpenAIFinitistic2026)].

Two further applications are developed in Section <a href="#sec:consequences" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:consequences">8</a>. The connection between generalized Nakayama failure and cotilting complements established by Buan and Solberg (Buan and Solberg 1998, Theorems 3.1 and 3.4) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[BuanSolberg1998](#ref-BuanSolberg1998)] gives a projective module over $\Gamma_K^{\mathrm{op}}$ that can be completed to a tilting module in infinitely many ways, each by adjoining one indecomposable summand; these summands are pairwise nonisomorphic (Corollary <a href="#cor:tilting-complements" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:tilting-complements">31</a>). Their introduction credits an independent proof of this connection to Happel and Unger (Buan and Solberg 1998, Introduction) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[BuanSolberg1998](#ref-BuanSolberg1998)]. The mirror-reflective constructions of Chen, Fang and Xi (Chen, Fang, and Xi 2024) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[ChenFangXi2024](#ref-ChenFangXi2024)] give auxiliary indecomposable symmetric algebras with arbitrarily long idempotent stratifications and the resulting chains of successive derived-category recollements specified in Corollary <a href="#cor:stratifying" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:stratifying">32</a>.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
###### Organization.

Section <a href="#sec:homological" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:homological">2</a> establishes the complete-resolution, duality, and finite-representative tools. Section <a href="#sec:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:transfer">3</a> proves the transfer to a symmetric algebra. Sections <a href="#a:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:section">4</a> and <a href="#e:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="e:section">5</a> construct the starting algebra and compute the stable self-maps and twist actions. Section <a href="#b:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="b:section">6</a> constructs the finite bimodule and proves the trace obstruction. Section <a href="#sec:triangular" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:triangular">7</a> forms the triangular module, establishes the complete vanishing profile, and applies the transfer to prove Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. Section <a href="#sec:consequences" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:consequences">8</a> studies the associated endomorphism algebra and proves Corollaries <a href="#cor:homological" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:homological">2</a> and <a href="#cor:finitistic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:finitistic">3</a>.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
###### Conventions.

All algebras are associative and unital, all modules are unital left modules unless indicated otherwise, and all modules occurring in the construction are finite-dimensional. Unspecified tensor products are over $k$, and $G^e=G\otimes_k G^{\mathrm{op}}$ acts on $G$-bimodules by the usual outer actions. Complexes are indexed homologically, with $P[a]_j=P_{j-a}$. Since the field has characteristic two, signs in their differentials and comparison maps disappear. The following section fixes the stable and derived conventions used throughout.

<a id="sec:homological"></a>
### Homological preliminaries

The tools in this section serve two passages in the construction. Complete-resolution comparison will turn the triangular cone’s endomorphism calculation into the vanishing needed for transfer to a symmetric algebra. Stable duality will supply the negative-degree maps used in that calculation. Separately, projective models and their stable images will turn the derived-induction object into a finite bimodule, preserving its maps and connecting morphisms under evaluation.

<a id="h:full-hom"></a>
Throughout the construction, $k$ has characteristic two. Unless explicitly stated otherwise, algebras and modules are finite dimensional over $k$. Resolution and model complexes have finite-dimensional terms; the full Hom complexes defined below may have infinite-dimensional product terms. Modules are left modules unless a side is specified. Write $D=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_k(-,k)$ and $G^e=G\otimes_kG^{\mathrm{op}}$; an unsubscripted tensor product is over $k$. Differentials lower homological degree, and $$P[a]_j=P_{j-a},\qquad c_jP=\mathop{\mathrm{coker}}(d^P_{j+1}:P_{j+1}\longrightarrow P_j).$$ Characteristic two removes the signs in shifts, tensor differentials and cones. For two complexes we use the full Hom complex $$\label{h:full-hom}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_G^a(P,Q)=\prod_{j\in\mathbb Z}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_G(P_j,Q_{j-a}),\qquad
 (\delta f)_j=d^Q_{j-a}f_j+f_{j-1}d^P_j.$$ For a module $N$, viewed in degree zero, $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_G(P,N)^a=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_G(P_a,N)$. The space $\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_G(M,N)$ is the quotient of $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_G(M,N)$ by maps factoring through finite projectives.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Complete resolutions and stable duality

An exact complex $P$ of projectives is *totally acyclic* if $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_G(P,G)$ is exact. If $c_0P=M$, we call $P$ a complete resolution of $M$. The comparison below also applies when $G$ is not self-injective. Complete resolutions and passage from derived objects to stable modules belong to the framework developed by Buchweitz (Buchweitz 2021) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[Buchweitz2021](#ref-Buchweitz2021)]. We give the comparison and truncation arguments in our conventions.

<a id="stmt-h-comparison"></a>
<div id="h:comparison" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Complete-resolution comparison). *Let $P$ be totally acyclic and let $Q$ be an exact complex of projectives. For every integer $a$ there are natural isomorphisms <a id="h:comparison-formula"></a>$$\label{h:comparison-formula}
 H^a\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_G^\bullet(P,Q)
 \simeq\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_G(c_aP,c_0Q)
 \simeq H^a\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_G(P,c_0Q).$$ The first isomorphism is induced by cokernels of shifted chain maps and respects composition. For $a>0$ these spaces are $\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_G^a(c_0P,c_0Q)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-comparison</summary>

*Proof.* We first record the extension property supplied by total acyclicity. If $J$ is projective, then $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_G(P,J)$ is exact, since $J$ is a summand of a finite sum of copies of $G$. Identify $c_jP$ with $\mathop{\mathrm{im}}d_j^P\subset P_{j-1}$. For $u:c_jP\to J$, the map $u d_j^P:P_j\to J$ is a cocycle. Exactness gives $h:P_{j-1}\to J$ with $h d_j^P=u d_j^P$, so $h$ extends $u$. It follows at once that the cycles of $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_G(P,N)^a$ are maps $c_aP\to N$ and its boundaries are exactly the maps factoring through projectives. This proves the second isomorphism, with $N=c_0Q$.

For the first, begin in degree zero. A map $c_0P\to c_0Q$ lifts to $f_0:P_0\to Q_0$, and then successively to $f_j$ for $j>0$, using projectivity of $P_j$ and exactness of $Q$. To proceed downwards, $d_j^Qf_j$ vanishes on $\ker d_j^P=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}d_{j+1}^P$ by the chain equation already obtained in degree $j+1$. It therefore defines a map $c_jP=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}d_j^P\to Q_{j-1}$. The extension property gives $f_{j-1}$. Thus every cokernel map lifts to a full chain map.

A homotopy induces a projective factorization on $c_0$. Conversely, suppose the cokernel map of $f$ factors as $c_0P\to J\to c_0Q$ with $J$ projective. Extend its first factor to $P_{-1}$ and lift its second factor to $Q_0$. Their composite is a homotopy component $h_{-1}:P_{-1}\to Q_0$ whose homotopy differential removes the induced map on cokernels. It remains to nullhomotope a chain map inducing zero there. For this remaining map set $h_{-1}=0$. For $j\geq0$, lift $$f_j+h_{j-1}d_j^P:P_j\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{im}}d_{j+1}^Q$$ to $h_j:P_j\to Q_{j+1}$; the initial image assertion is the vanishing on $c_0$, and each later one follows from the preceding chain equation. For $j<0$, the map $f_j+d_{j+1}^Qh_j$ kills $\mathop{\mathrm{im}}d_{j+1}^P$ by the equation in degree $j+1$. It descends to $\mathop{\mathrm{im}}d_j^P$ and extends to $h_{j-1}:P_{j-1}\to Q_j$ by the same extension property. These equations give $f=dh+hd$ in every degree. The product in <a href="#h:full-hom" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:full-hom">[h:full-hom]</a> permits all these components; each equation uses only two of them.

Replace $P$ by $P[-a]$ to obtain degree $a$. The construction by cokernels preserves compositions and shifts. Finally, the nonnegative tail of $P$ resolves $c_0P$. In every positive cohomological degree its Hom complex has the same two adjacent differentials as $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_G(P,c_0Q)$, proving the Ext assertion, including degree one. ◻

</details>

Recall that $G$ is *symmetric* if there is a bimodule isomorphism $G\simeq DG$, or equivalently a linear form $t:G\to k$ such that $t(ab)=t(ba)$ and $(a,b)\mapsto t(ab)$ is nondegenerate. In this case finite projectives are injective, and every finite module embeds in a finite projective: dualize a finite projective surjection onto its dual. Splicing a projective resolution and a projective-injective coresolution therefore gives a complete resolution of every finite module; applying $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_G(-,G)$ preserves its exactness because $G$ is injective.

<a id="h:syzygy-sequence"></a>
<a id="h:stable-ext"></a>
For such a resolution put $M[a]=c_{-a}P$ in the stable category. The exact sequences $$\label{h:syzygy-sequence}
 0\longrightarrow c_{j+1}P\longrightarrow P_j
 \longrightarrow c_jP\longrightarrow0$$ show that $[1]$ is inverse syzygy. Lemma <a href="#h:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:comparison">4</a> makes these shifts independent of choices, functorial on stable maps, and compatible with composition. In particular, $$\label{h:stable-ext}
 H^a\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_G(P,N)=\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_G(M,N[a])\qquad(a\in\mathbb Z).$$ For positive degrees the shifted composition is the Yoneda product, since both are computed by lifting maps to projective resolutions.

A short exact sequence $0\to L\to V\to N\to0$ gives a long exact sequence of the groups in <a href="#h:stable-ext" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:stable-ext">[h:stable-ext]</a>: apply $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_G(P,-)$ termwise. Its connecting map is composition with $\partial:N\to L[1]$. Concretely, lift a projective surjection $J\to N$ to $J\to V$ and restrict to its kernel. The resulting map $\Omega N\to L$, shifted once, is $\partial$. This agrees with the cochain connecting map, which lifts a cocycle and then applies the differential. We use this description to keep track of connecting maps, as well as the groups in the long exact sequences.

The next lemma is the symmetric form of Auslander–Reiten duality, also called Tate duality; see (Linckelmann 2013, secs. 2, (2.3) and (2.7)–(2.8)) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[Linckelmann2013](#ref-Linckelmann2013)]. Its composition pairing will determine the action on negative-degree self-maps.

<a id="stmt-h-duality"></a>
<div id="h:duality" class="lemma">

<a id="h:shifted-pairing"></a>
**Lemma 5** (Symmetric stable duality). *For a symmetric algebra $G$ there are natural isomorphisms <a id="h:duality-formula"></a>$$\label{h:duality-formula}
 D\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_G(M,N)\simeq\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_G(N,M[-1]).$$ There is a linear functional $\lambda_M:\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_G(M,M[-1])\to k$ for which the perfect pairing is $(f,g)\mapsto\lambda_M(gf)$. Consequently, for every integer $a$ the pairing $$\label{h:shifted-pairing}
 \underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_G(M,N[a])\times\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_G(N,M[-a-1])\longrightarrow k,
 \qquad (\alpha,\beta)\longmapsto\lambda_M\bigl(\beta[a]\alpha\bigr)$$ is perfect and natural.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-duality"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-duality</summary>

*Proof.* For every finite projective $J$ the finite-dimensional adjunction gives $$D\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_G(J,N)\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_G(N,DG\otimes_GJ)\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_G(N,J).$$ The first identification is immediate for $J=G$, hence for finite sums and summands; the second uses a fixed symmetrizing form. Apply this termwise to a complete resolution $P$ of $M$. Dualizing degree-zero cohomology of $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_G(P,N)$ yields $$\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_G\bigl(N,\ker(P_0\to P_{-1})\bigr)}
 {\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_G(N,P_1)\to
                \mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_G(N,\ker(P_0\to P_{-1}))\bigr)}.$$ Here $\ker(P_0\to P_{-1})=c_1P=M[-1]$. A factorization through any projective lifts through $P_1\twoheadrightarrow c_1P$, so the displayed quotient is precisely $\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_G(N,M[-1])$. All identifications are natural; comparison of complete resolutions also gives naturality in $M$.

Define $\lambda_M(g)$ by pairing $1_M$ with $g$. Naturality in $N$, applied to $f:M\to N$, identifies the pairing of $f$ and $g$ with $\lambda_M(gf)$. Replacing $N$ by $N[a]$ and using the shift equivalences gives <a href="#h:shifted-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:shifted-pairing">[h:shifted-pairing]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Projective models and their stable images

In Section <a href="#b:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="b:section">6</a>, derived induction first supplies a bounded derived bimodule. We will obtain the finite bimodule $Y$ from high cokernels of a projective model. To compute its evaluated stable Hom groups, we must also retain the actual maps attaching the model’s successive pieces. The next two lemmas establish this passage from derived objects and connecting morphisms to finite stable modules.

We write $\mathrm D^b(G)$ for the derived category of bounded complexes of finite modules. A complex is *perfect* if it is derived isomorphic to a bounded complex of finite projectives. We will represent bounded derived objects by bounded-below complexes of finite projectives with bounded homology. The following elementary facts specify the models and maps we use. For a chain map $f:L\to N$, our cone convention is <a id="h:cone"></a>$$\label{h:cone}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Cone}}(f)_j=N_j\oplus L_{j-1},\qquad
 d_j^{\mathop{\mathrm{Cone}}(f)}=
 \begin{pmatrix}d_j^N&f_{j-1}\\0&d_{j-1}^L\end{pmatrix}.$$

The projective-model statement is the bounded case of the standard $K$-projective formalism; see (Spaltenstein 1988, Proposition 1.4 and Example 3.2(a)) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[Spaltenstein1988](#ref-Spaltenstein1988)], with the opposite indexing convention for complexes.

<a id="stmt-h-derived-models"></a>
<div id="h:derived-models" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6** (Derived maps and two-cell models). *A bounded-below complex $P$ of projectives computes derived maps out of it by chain maps modulo homotopy. Every bounded complex of finite modules has such a projective model with finite terms. In particular, two such models of the same derived object are homotopy equivalent.*

<a id="h:two-cell"></a>
*Suppose a derived triangle is given by $$L\longrightarrow V\longrightarrow R\xrightarrow{\partial}L[1].$$ For projective models $P_L,P_R$ and a chain map $b:P_R\to P_L[1]$ representing $\partial$, a model of $V$ is the two-cell complex $$\label{h:two-cell}
 P_{V,j}=P_{L,j}\oplus P_{R,j},\qquad
 d_j^{P_V}=\begin{pmatrix}d_j^{P_L}&b_j\\0&d_j^{P_R}\end{pmatrix}.$$ Its subcomplex and quotient represent $L$ and $R$, and its connecting map is the specified $\partial$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-derived-models"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-derived-models</summary>

*Proof.* Every chain map from $P$ to an acyclic complex is nullhomotopic: start at the lowest nonzero degree of $P$ and successively lift the residual map through the next differential of the target. The same argument applies to every shift. Thus the full Hom from $P$ sends quasi-isomorphisms to quasi-isomorphisms, which proves the assertion about derived maps. This is the $K$-projectivity of a bounded-below projective source; it does not assert $K$-projectivity of a complete resolution.

For completeness, a bounded-below finite complex $V$ admits a projective model $\theta:Q\to V$ by the following induction. Having constructed lower terms, choose $Q_j$ finite projective surjecting onto $$\{(x,y)\in V_j\oplus Q_{j-1}:d_Vx=\theta(y),\ d_Qy=0\}.$$ Use its two components for $\theta_j$ and $d_j^Q$. Every cycle of the comparison cone is then a boundary, so $\theta$ is a quasi-isomorphism. A derived isomorphism between projective models and its inverse are represented by chain maps, and their composites are homotopic to the identities by the first assertion.

To verify the last assertion, represent the first arrow of the triangle by $f:P_L\to P_V$ on any projective models. Its cone represents $R$, and the projection $\mathop{\mathrm{Cone}}(f)\to P_L[1]$ represents $\partial$. The cone of the deshifted projection has $P_V$ as a subcomplex with contractible quotient $\mathop{\mathrm{Cone}}(1_{P_L})$. It therefore represents $V$. Replacing the projective models by homotopy equivalent ones and the map by its prescribed representative preserves this conclusion: a homotopy commuting square induces a cone map with that homotopy as its off-diagonal component, and homotopic maps have isomorphic cones. The resulting cone $\mathop{\mathrm{Cone}}(b[-1])$ is exactly <a href="#h:two-cell" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:two-cell">[h:two-cell]</a>. ◻

</details>

A bounded-below complex with bounded homology represents a bounded derived object by good truncation. Also, a bounded complex whose terms have finite projective dimension is perfect: its finite filtration by terms expresses it as successive cones of shifts of modules with bounded projective resolutions. These observations allow us to use the preceding models without introducing infinite-dimensional terms.

<a id="stmt-h-stable-image"></a>
<div id="h:stable-image" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (Stable images). *Let $G$ be symmetric and let $P$ be bounded below with projective terms and bounded homology. For $n$ sufficiently large the formula <a id="h:stable-image-formula"></a>$$\label{h:stable-image-formula}
 \langle P\rangle=(c_nP)[n]$$ defines a stable module, independent of $n$ up to natural stable isomorphism. It is functorial on derived maps represented by such models, commutes with shifts, sends a module to its stable class, and sends an Ext class to its corresponding stable map.*

*A degreewise split short exact sequence $0\to P_L\to P_V\to P_R\to0$ of these complexes gives a stable connecting map $\langle P_R\rangle\to\langle P_L\rangle[1]$ and the associated long exact stable Hom sequences. With respect to a graded splitting, this connecting map is the stable image of the cross differential $b:P_R\to P_L[1]$ in <a href="#h:two-cell" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:two-cell">[h:two-cell]</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-stable-image"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-stable-image</summary>

*Proof.* Above the homology support, <a href="#h:syzygy-sequence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:syzygy-sequence">[h:syzygy-sequence]</a> is exact for $P$. It identifies $(c_{n+1}P)[n+1]$ with $(c_nP)[n]$, naturally on chain maps. A homotopy changes $c_n(f)$ by a map factoring through $P_{n-1}$, so the construction descends to homotopy classes. Lemma <a href="#h:derived-models" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:derived-models">6</a> then makes it functorial on derived maps. The identity $c_n(P[a])=c_{n-a}P$ gives shift compatibility. A projective resolution of a module recovers its successive syzygies, proving the assertion for modules. Restricting a lifted representative of an Ext class to high degrees gives the same successive syzygies of that map, proving the last assertion about classes.

Choose $n$ so that all three complexes are exact in degrees at least $n$. Their cokernels give a short exact sequence $$0\longrightarrow c_nP_L\longrightarrow c_nP_V
 \longrightarrow c_nP_R\longrightarrow0.$$ For injectivity on the left, suppose $(x,0)=d_{n+1}^{P_V}(u,v)$. Then $d_{n+1}^{P_R}v=0$, so $v=d_{n+2}^{P_R}w$ for some $w$. The equation $d_Lb+bd_R=0$ gives $x=d_{n+1}^{P_L}(u+b_{n+2}w)$, as required. Surjectivity and exactness in the middle follow by lifting components in the graded splitting.

There is a commutative diagram with exact rows $$\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large]
0\arrow[r]&c_{n+1}P_R\arrow[r]\arrow[d,"\overline b_{n+1}"']
 &P_{R,n}\arrow[r]\arrow[d]
 &c_nP_R\arrow[r]\arrow[d,equal]&0\\
0\arrow[r]&c_nP_L\arrow[r]&c_nP_V\arrow[r]&c_nP_R\arrow[r]&0.
\end{tikzcd}$$ The middle vertical map sends $z$ to the class of $(0,z)$. On an element $d_{n+1}^{P_R}z$, its value is the class of $(b_{n+1}z,0)$, since $(b_{n+1}z,d_{n+1}^{P_R}z)$ is a boundary in $P_V$. Thus the left vertical map is induced by $b_{n+1}:P_{R,n+1}\to P_{L,n}$. Shifting the left vertical arrow by $[n+1]$ gives $\langle P_R\rangle\to\langle P_L\rangle[1]$. By the description after <a href="#h:stable-ext" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:stable-ext">[h:stable-ext]</a>, this is the connecting map of the bottom row shifted by $[n]$, and it is exactly $\langle b\rangle$. This also gives the asserted long exact sequences. ◻

</details>

The following criterion supplies the other use of stable images in Section <a href="#b:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="b:section">6</a>. There, an ordinary trace will rule out a perfect factorization of a derived map. The criterion then shows that its stable image is nonzero.

<a id="stmt-h-perfect-factor"></a>
<div id="h:perfect-factor" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8** (Vanishing stable image and perfect factorization). *Over a symmetric algebra, a derived map between bounded derived objects whose stable image is zero factors in the derived category through a perfect complex.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-perfect-factor"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-perfect-factor</summary>

*Proof.* Choose bounded-below projective models $P,Q$ and represent the map by a global chain map $f:P\to Q$, using Lemma <a href="#h:derived-models" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:derived-models">6</a>. For a sufficiently high $n$, both complexes are exact in degrees at least $n$ and $c_n(f)$ factors through a finite projective $J$: $$c_nP\xrightarrow{u}J\xrightarrow{v}c_nQ.$$ Since $J$ is injective, extend $u$ across $c_nP=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}d_n^P\subset P_{n-1}$. Since $J$ is projective, lift $v$ to $Q_n$. The composite gives $h_{n-1}:P_{n-1}\to Q_n$. It makes $f_n+h_{n-1}d_n^P$ zero after projection to $c_nQ$, so this residual map lifts through $d_{n+1}^Q$ to $h_n:P_n\to Q_{n+1}$. Inductively, for $j>n$ the chain equation and the preceding homotopy equation show that $f_j+h_{j-1}d_j^P$ lands in $\ker d_j^Q=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}d_{j+1}^Q$. Lift it to $h_j$. Set $h_j=0$ for $j<n-1$.

<a id="h:truncation-boundary"></a>
The homotopic map $f'=f+dh+hd$ now vanishes in every degree $j\geq n$. Its chain equation in degree $n$ reads $$\label{h:truncation-boundary}
 f'_{n-1}d_n^P=0.$$ Consequently its remaining components define a chain map into the actual homological subcomplex $Q_{\leq n-1}$, obtained by retaining $Q_j$ for $j\leq n-1$ and setting the higher terms to zero. Equation <a href="#h:truncation-boundary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:truncation-boundary">[h:truncation-boundary]</a> verifies the boundary condition at the cut. Thus $f'$ factors through $Q_{\leq n-1}$, which is bounded and projective because $Q$ was bounded below. Composing with the chosen model maps gives the desired derived factorization. This also covers a map with a module as target: first lift it globally to a projective resolution of that module, and then apply this argument. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Bimodule evaluation

For a symmetric algebra $E$, the tensor product of symmetrizing forms makes $E^e$ symmetric. We call an $E$-bimodule *side-projective* if it is projective as a left and as a right $E$-module, and write $LX=L\otimes_EX$. Side-projectivity does not assert projectivity over $E^e$. For the derived-induction construction, the passage to a finite stable bimodule must commute with evaluation at $X$, including the attaching maps just discussed. The next lemma establishes these compatibilities.

<a id="stmt-h-evaluation"></a>
<div id="h:evaluation" class="lemma">

<a id="h:evaluation-shifts"></a>
**Lemma 9** (Finite shifts and evaluation). *Every stable shift of a finite side-projective bimodule has a finite side-projective representative. Evaluation at a finite $E$-module $X$ sends stable maps between these bimodules to stable $E$-module maps and has natural identifications $$\label{h:evaluation-shifts}
 (L[a])X\simeq(LX)[a],\qquad L(X[a])\simeq(LX)[a].$$ It preserves the stable connecting maps of short exact sequences of side-projective bimodules, and these identifications are natural in both $L$ and $X$.*

<a id="h:evaluation-image"></a>
*If $P$ is a bounded-below complex of projective bimodules with bounded homology and all sufficiently high $c_nP$ are side-projective, then $P\otimes_EX$ has bounded homology and $$\label{h:evaluation-image}
 \langle P\rangle X\simeq\langle P\otimes_EX\rangle,$$ naturally on maps and on connecting maps from degreewise split sequences of such complexes. The hypothesis on the high cokernels holds whenever $P$, considered separately on either side, is homotopy equivalent to a bounded complex of projectives on that side.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-evaluation"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-evaluation</summary>

*Proof.* Take a projective bimodule surjection onto $L$. Its kernel is side-projective because the sequence splits on each side. For a positive shift, use the finite projective-injective embedding $$L\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_k(E^e,L),\qquad
 z\longmapsto(a\longmapsto az),
 \qquad (r\varphi)(a)=\varphi(ar).$$ Its target is injective by coinduction and projective by symmetry of $E^e$. The embedding splits on each side because $L$ is injective on that side, so its cokernel is again side-projective. Iteration supplies finite representatives for every shift.

An $E^e$-projective evaluates to an $E$-projective: this is immediate for the free bimodule $E\otimes E$, which evaluates to $E\otimes X$, and passes to finite sums and summands. Hence evaluation respects projective factorizations. Every short exact sequence of side-projective bimodules splits on the right, and therefore remains exact after evaluation. In particular, evaluating the sequences just used to define shifts gives the first identification in <a href="#h:evaluation-shifts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:evaluation-shifts">[h:evaluation-shifts]</a>. Applying the projective-presentation description of connecting maps to these same sequences shows that their actual connecting maps are preserved.

For fixed $L$, right projectivity makes $L\otimes_E-$ exact, and left projectivity makes it preserve projectives. Applying it to projective resolutions and projective-injective coresolutions gives the second identification in <a href="#h:evaluation-shifts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:evaluation-shifts">[h:evaluation-shifts]</a>. A bimodule map $L\to L'$ gives a natural transformation on these complexes; thus evaluating it on a shifted module agrees stably with shifting its evaluation. The same reasoning proves naturality in $X$ and compatibility with composition.

For the complex assertion, the high exact sequences $0\to c_{n+1}P\to P_n\to c_nP\to0$ split on the right. Their evaluations remain exact, proving bounded homology of $P\otimes_EX$; its terms are projective by the preceding argument. Right exactness gives the canonical identifications $$c_n(P\otimes_EX)=(c_nP)\otimes_EX.$$ Together with <a href="#h:evaluation-shifts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:evaluation-shifts">[h:evaluation-shifts]</a>, these identify the two sides of <a href="#h:evaluation-image" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:evaluation-image">[h:evaluation-image]</a>. They commute with chain maps. For a filtered complex, the cross differential in Lemma <a href="#h:stable-image" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:stable-image">7</a> evaluates to its tensor with $X$. That lemma’s explicit diagram therefore identifies the evaluated connecting map with the connecting map after evaluation.

Finally, suppose a homotopy equivalence on one side compares $P$ with a complex supported in degrees at most $d$. At any $n>d$, the homotopy between the identity and the composite through that complex gives $$1_{P_n}=d_{n+1}h_n+h_{n-1}d_n.$$ Writing $\pi_n:P_n\to c_nP$, passage to the cokernel yields $$1_{c_nP}=(\pi_nh_{n-1})\overline d_n,
 \qquad \overline d_n:c_nP\longrightarrow P_{n-1}.$$ Thus $c_nP$ is a summand of a projective on that side. The separate homotopy equivalence on the other side proves the other projectivity. The homotopies need not be bimodule homotopies. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:transfer"></a>
### Passing to a symmetric algebra

The final step of the construction has a general form. It explains why we will need vanishing in negative degrees as well as positive ones. Throughout this section, $R$ is a finite-dimensional algebra over our field $k$ and $D=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_k(-,k)$.

We recall the standard symmetry of a trivial extension; see (Bessenrodt, Holm, and Zimmermann 2007, Proposition 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[BessenrodtHolmZimmermann2007](#ref-BessenrodtHolmZimmermann2007)].

<a id="stmt-tr-trivial-symmetric"></a>
<div id="tr:trivial-symmetric" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10**. *The trivial extension $R\ltimes DR$ is a finite-dimensional symmetric algebra. In particular, its left and right regular modules are injective in the categories of all unital modules.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-tr-trivial-symmetric"><summary>Proof of stmt-tr-trivial-symmetric</summary>

*Proof.* The dual bimodule structure is $$(a\lambda b)(c)=\lambda(bca)
 \qquad(a,b,c\in R,\ \lambda\in DR),$$ and the multiplication is $$(a,\lambda)(b,\mu)=(ab,a\mu+\lambda b).$$ The bimodule identities give associativity, and $(1,0)$ is a unit. The functional $t(a,\lambda)=\lambda(1)$ gives the pairing $$t\big((a,\lambda)(b,\mu)\big)=\mu(a)+\lambda(b).$$ This pairing is symmetric and nondegenerate, and associativity of the algebra makes it an associative form. Thus $R\ltimes DR$ is isomorphic to its dual as a bimodule.

For any algebra $G$ over $k$, the natural isomorphism $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_G(L,DG)\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_k(L,k)$ shows that $DG$ is an injective left $G$-module; the same argument applies on the right. This statement holds for arbitrary unital $L$, since vector-space duality is exact. Applying it to the symmetric algebra $G=R\ltimes DR$ proves the final assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-tr-transfer"></a>
<div id="tr:transfer" class="proposition">

<a id="tr:two-sided-vanishing"></a>
**Proposition 11** (Transfer through the trivial extension). *Let $P$ be a totally acyclic complex of finite projective left $R$-modules, and put $Z=c_0P$. Suppose that $Z$ is nonprojective and $$\label{tr:two-sided-vanishing}
 H^a\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_R(P,Z)=0
 \qquad\text{for }a>0\text{ and for }a\leq -2.$$ Then $$A=R\ltimes DR,\qquad M=A\otimes_R Z$$ are a finite-dimensional symmetric algebra and a finite-dimensional nonprojective left $A$-module satisfying $$\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_A^a(M,M)=0\qquad(a>0).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-tr-transfer"><summary>Proof of stmt-tr-transfer</summary>

*Proof.* Write $\nu=DR\otimes_R-$ and set $I=\nu P$. Finite projectivity gives natural isomorphisms <a id="tr:nakayama-evaluation"></a>$$\label{tr:nakayama-evaluation}
 DR\otimes_R P_j\simeq D\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_R(P_j,R),
 \qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_R(Z,DR\otimes_R P_j)\simeq D\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_R(P_j,Z).$$ Both follow first for finite free modules and then for their direct summands. The module $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_R(P_j,R)$ is finite projective on the right, so $I_j$ is injective on the left. Total acyclicity of $P$ and exactness of $D$ show that the entire complex $I$ is exact.

As a right $R$-module, $A=R\oplus DR$. Consequently the restriction of $A\otimes_RP$ is the exact complex $P\oplus I$. Every term $A\otimes_RP_j$ is finite projective over $A$, since induction preserves a split direct-summand presentation. Right exactness identifies $c_0(A\otimes_RP)$ with $A\otimes_RZ=M$. Thus the nonnegative tail of $A\otimes_RP$ is a projective resolution of $M$. In particular, the tensor exactness needed here has been proved for this resolution; flatness of $A_R$ is not an additional hypothesis.

<a id="tr:injective-tail"></a>
<a id="tr:negative-duality"></a>
Put $N=\nu Z$. Again by right exactness, $N=c_0I$. Exactness of $I$ identifies this cokernel with $\mathop{\mathrm{im}}(I_0\to I_{-1})$. Hence $$\label{tr:injective-tail}
 0\longrightarrow N\longrightarrow I_{-1}
 \longrightarrow I_{-2}\longrightarrow I_{-3}\longrightarrow\cdots$$ is an injective coresolution. If $C^j=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_R(P_j,Z)$ with differential $\delta^j:C^j\to C^{j+1}$, applying $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_R(Z,-)$ to <a href="#tr:injective-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:injective-tail">[tr:injective-tail]</a> gives, in cohomological degrees $0,1,2$, $$DC^{-1}\xrightarrow{D\delta^{-2}}DC^{-2}
 \xrightarrow{D\delta^{-3}}DC^{-3}.$$ Both arrows surrounding degree one are present. More generally, the term in degree $a\geq0$ is $DC^{-a-1}$. Thus, for every $a>0$, $$\label{tr:negative-duality}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_R^a(Z,N)\simeq D H^{-a-1}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_R(P,Z)=0.$$ The first of these equalities uses degree $-2$, which accounts for the endpoint in <a href="#tr:two-sided-vanishing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:two-sided-vanishing">[tr:two-sided-vanishing]</a>.

Induction–restriction adjunction on the projective resolution already constructed now yields $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_A^a(M,M)
 &\simeq \mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_R^a\bigl(Z,\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_R^A M\bigr)\\
 &\simeq \mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_R^a(Z,Z)\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_R^a(Z,N)=0
 \qquad(a>0).
\end{aligned}$$ Here the first summand vanishes by <a href="#tr:two-sided-vanishing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:two-sided-vanishing">[tr:two-sided-vanishing]</a> and Lemma <a href="#h:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:comparison">4</a>, and the second by <a href="#tr:negative-duality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:negative-duality">[tr:negative-duality]</a>.

Lemma <a href="#tr:trivial-symmetric" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tr:trivial-symmetric">10</a> proves that $A$ is symmetric. The module $M$ is finite and unital. Finally, if $M$ were a direct summand of $A^r$, tensoring the splitting with the quotient $A\to R$ would make $$R\otimes_A M\simeq R\otimes_A(A\otimes_RZ)\simeq Z$$ a direct summand of $R^r$. A tensor functor preserves a split identity composite without any flatness assumption. This contradicts nonprojectivity of $Z$. ◻

</details>

We will apply Proposition <a href="#tr:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tr:transfer">11</a> to a triangular algebra $R=\Lambda$. The rest of the proof constructs its module $Z$ together with the complete resolution and the two vanishing ranges in <a href="#tr:two-sided-vanishing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:two-sided-vanishing">[tr:two-sided-vanishing]</a>.

<a id="a:section"></a>
### The ten-dimensional algebra

We now construct an algebra $C$ and a simple module $s$ whose resolution supplies the degree-three extension classes used below. Besides this resolution, we need finite projective-dimension bounds for the dual regular modules; these bounds make derived induction admit a finite bimodule representative. The multiplication of $C$ is the one in (OpenAI 2026b, sec. 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[OpenAIAR2026](#ref-OpenAIAR2026)]; all properties needed here are proved directly. We work over the fixed field $k=\mathbb F_2(q,H_1,H_2)$, where the three parameters are algebraically independent.

<a id="a:corners"></a>
<a id="a:multiplication"></a>
Let $C$ have the following basis, arranged by its left and right idempotents: $$\begin{array}{c|c@{\qquad}c|c}
\text{corner}&\text{basis}&\text{corner}&\text{basis}\\ \hline
eCe&e,x,y,z&eCf&u,v\\
fCe&t,j&fCf&f,n.
\end{array}
\label{a:corners}$$ The unit is $1=e+f$. The complete multiplication table, with the left factor indexing the row, is $$\begingroup
\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}
\begin{array}{c|cccccccccc}
 &e&f&x&y&z&u&v&t&j&n\\ \hline
e&e&0&x&y&z&u&v&0&0&0\\
f&0&f&0&0&0&0&0&t&j&n\\
x&x&0&0&qz&0&v&0&0&0&0\\
y&y&0&z&0&0&v&0&0&0&0\\
z&z&0&0&0&0&0&0&0&0&0\\
u&0&u&0&0&0&0&0&y+qx&z&v\\
v&0&v&0&0&0&0&0&qz&0&0\\
t&t&0&j&q^2j&0&(1+q)n&0&0&0&0\\
j&j&0&0&0&0&0&0&0&0&0\\
n&0&n&0&0&0&0&0&qj&0&0
\end{array}
\endgroup
\label{a:multiplication}$$

<a id="stmt-a-table"></a>
<div id="a:table" class="lemma">

**Lemma 12**. *The table <a href="#a:multiplication" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:multiplication">[a:multiplication]</a> defines an associative unital algebra of dimension ten. Its radical is the span of the eight basis vectors other than $e,f$, and $(\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}C)^5=0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-a-table"><summary>Proof of stmt-a-table</summary>

*Proof.* Give the basis vectors the degrees $$\begin{gathered}
\deg e=\deg f=0,\qquad \deg u=\deg t=1,\\
\deg x=\deg y=\deg n=2,\qquad
\deg v=\deg j=3,\qquad \deg z=4.
\end{gathered}$$ The table is homogeneous and respects the corners. Thus associativity with an idempotent, or with incompatible adjacent corners, follows from <a href="#a:corners" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:corners">[a:corners]</a>. For positive-degree triples of total degree greater than four, both bracketings vanish. The remaining composable triples and the value of either bracketing are precisely $$\begin{array}{c|ccccc}
abc&utu&tut&xut&yut&ntu\\ \hline
(ab)c=a(bc)&(1+q)v&q(1+q)j&qz&qz&0
\end{array}$$ $$\begin{array}{c|cccccc}
abc&unt&txu&tyu&utx&uty&tun\\ \hline
(ab)c=a(bc)&qz&0&0&z&q^2z&0.
\end{array}$$ Every entry is obtained by two applications of <a href="#a:multiplication" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:multiplication">[a:multiplication]</a>; this exhausts the associativity check by trilinearity. The positive-degree span is a nilpotent ideal with fifth power zero and quotient $ke\oplus kf\cong k^2$. It is therefore the radical. ◻

</details>

Let $s$ be the one-dimensional left $C$-module on which $f$ acts as the identity and every other basis vector acts as zero. Its vector space dual $Ds$ is the right simple with the same character.

The corner $eCe$ gives a concrete connection with Schulz’s example (Schulz 1995, Example 7) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[Schulz1995](#ref-Schulz1995)]. The table identifies it with the quantum exterior algebra $$eCe\cong k\langle x,y\rangle/(x^2,y^2,yx-q^{-1}xy).$$ Moreover, the assignments $t\mapsto x+q^{-1}y$ and $j\mapsto q^{-1}z$ give an isomorphism of right $eCe$-modules $$fCe\cong(x+q^{-1}y)eCe.$$ The identities $tx=j$, $ty=q^2j$, and $jx=jy=0$ verify right-linearity, and the two images form a basis of the right ideal. With Schulz’s parameter $p=q^{-1}$, this right ideal is the first syzygy of his module $(x+y)eCe$. The later left-module resolutions will use the same type of parameter shift within the ten-dimensional algebra.

<a id="a:trivial-extension"></a>
Write $a^*$ for the basis vector of $DC$ dual to a basis vector $a$ of $C$. Form the trivial extension $$T=C\ltimes DC,\qquad
(a,\phi)(b,\psi)=(ab,a\psi+\phi b),\qquad
(a\phi b)(c)=\phi(bca).
\label{a:trivial-extension}$$ By Lemma <a href="#tr:trivial-symmetric" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tr:trivial-symmetric">10</a>, this is a symmetric algebra of dimension twenty, with symmetrizing functional $t_T(a,\phi)=\phi(1)$. In particular, $$t_T\bigl((a,\phi)(b,\psi)\bigr)=\phi(b)+\psi(a).$$ We regard $s$ as a $T$-module through the quotient $T\to C$. Set <a id="a:tensor-algebras"></a>$$B=C\otimes C\subseteq E=T\otimes T.
\label{a:tensor-algebras}$$ Then $\dim_k B=100$ and $\dim_k E=400$. The functional $t_E=t_T\otimes t_T$ gives a symmetric nondegenerate product pairing, so $E$ is symmetric.

<a id="a:radicals"></a>
<a id="a:corner-parity"></a>
We record the radical and corner information that will enter the trace argument. One has $$\begin{aligned}
\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}T&=(\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}C)\oplus DC,\nonumber\\
\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}B&=(\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}C)\otimes C+C\otimes(\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}C),\nonumber\\
\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}E&=(\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}T)\otimes T+T\otimes(\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}T).
\label{a:radicals}
\end{aligned}$$ For the first equality, the displayed ideal is a square-zero extension of the nilpotent ideal $\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}C$, and its quotient is $k^2$. For the tensor products the displayed ideals are sums of commuting nilpotent ideals, and their quotients are $k^4$. This proves all three equalities. The four vertex idempotents of $B$, also used in $E$, are $e\otimes e,e\otimes f,f\otimes e,f\otimes f$. For these vertices the corner dimensions are $$\bigl(\dim_k(pB p')\bigr)_{p,p'}=
\begin{pmatrix}
16&8&8&4\\
8&8&4&4\\
8&4&8&4\\
4&4&4&4
\end{pmatrix}.
\label{a:corner-parity}$$ Indeed these dimensions are the products of the corresponding corner dimensions $4,2,2,2$ of $C$. In particular, every entry is even.

For $i\in\mathbb Z$, put $$\ell_i=x+q^i y.$$ We denote right multiplication by $a$ by $\rho_a$ and left multiplication by $a$ by $\lambda_a$. Thus $\rho_a$ is a map of left modules and $\lambda_a$ is a map of right modules whenever the indicated source and target corners allow it.

<a id="stmt-a-dual-dimensions"></a>
<div id="a:dual-dimensions" class="proposition">

<a id="a:left-dual-resolution"></a>
<a id="a:right-dual-resolution"></a>
<a id="a:dimension-bounds"></a>
**Proposition 13**. *The dual regular module $DC$ has projective dimension at most two on both sides. More precisely, $$D(eC)\cong Ce,\qquad D(Ce)\cong eC,$$ and there are exact sequences $$\begin{aligned}
0\longrightarrow Cf\xrightarrow{\rho_t}Ce
 \xrightarrow{\rho_{\ell_{-2}}}Ce
 \xrightarrow{e\mapsto j^*}D(fC)\longrightarrow0,
\label{a:left-dual-resolution}\\
0\longrightarrow fC\xrightarrow{\lambda_u}eC
 \xrightarrow{\lambda_{\ell_0}}eC
 \xrightarrow{e\mapsto v^*}D(Cf)\longrightarrow0.
\label{a:right-dual-resolution}
\end{aligned}$$ Consequently $$\mathop{\mathrm{pd}}_B({}_B E)\le4,\qquad
\mathop{\mathrm{pd}}_{B^{\mathrm{op}}}(E_B)\le4,\qquad
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}_B({}_B B)\le4.
\label{a:dimension-bounds}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-a-dual-dimensions"><summary>Proof of stmt-a-dual-dimensions</summary>

*Proof.* The pairing $eC\times Ce\to k$ that takes the coefficient of $z$ in a product is nondegenerate. In the ordered bases $$\mathcal B_{eC}=(e,x,y,z,u,v),\qquad
\mathcal B_{Ce}=(e,x,y,z,t,j),$$ its only nonzero entries are the products $ez,xy,yx,ze,uj,vt$, with coefficients $1,q,1,1,1,q$. Thus it has determinant $q^2\ne0$. The maps sending $e$ to $z^*$ give the stated left- and right-module isomorphisms.

Here is the full calculation for the other dual summands. The two surjections, on the ordered bases just specified, have the images $$\begin{aligned}
Ce\longrightarrow D(fC):\quad
(e,x,y,z,t,j)&\longmapsto
(j^*,t^*,q^2t^*,0,qn^*,f^*),\\
eC\longrightarrow D(Cf):\quad
(e,x,y,z,u,v)&\longmapsto
(v^*,u^*,u^*,0,n^*,f^*).
\end{aligned}$$ Each has rank four; their kernels are respectively $\langle\ell_{-2},z\rangle_k$ and $\langle\ell_0,z\rangle_k$. The intermediate maps have images and kernels $$\begin{array}{c|c|c}
\text{map}&\text{image}&\text{kernel}\\ \hline
\rho_{\ell_{-2}}:Ce\to Ce
 &\langle\ell_{-2},z\rangle_k
 &\langle y+qx,z,t,j\rangle_k\\
\lambda_{\ell_0}:eC\to eC
 &\langle\ell_0,z\rangle_k
 &\langle y+qx,z,u,v\rangle_k.
\end{array}$$ For completeness, the initial injections are given by $$\begin{aligned}
\rho_t:(f,u,v,n)&\longmapsto(t,y+qx,qz,qj),\\
\lambda_u:(f,t,j,n)&\longmapsto(u,y+qx,z,v).
\end{aligned}$$ Their four images are independent and span the respective kernels in the table. This proves exactness of <a href="#a:left-dual-resolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:left-dual-resolution">[a:left-dual-resolution]</a> and <a href="#a:right-dual-resolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:right-dual-resolution">[a:right-dual-resolution]</a>, including their orientations and the exceptional index $-2$ in the first sequence.

On either $C$-side, $T=C\oplus DC$ has a projective resolution of length at most two. Tensor two such resolutions over the field and totalize. The resulting resolutions of the corresponding $B$-modules $E$ have length at most four, proving the first two bounds in <a href="#a:dimension-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:dimension-bounds">[a:dimension-bounds]</a>. Likewise $DB\cong DC\otimes DC$ has right $B$-projective dimension at most four. Dualizing a finite projective resolution gives an injective coresolution of ${}_B B$, and proves the last bound. ◻

</details>

We now resolve $s$. The same multiplication maps will also determine the dual complex, including its right-module cohomology.

<a id="stmt-a-resolution"></a>
<div id="a:resolution" class="proposition">

<a id="a:simple-resolution"></a>
<a id="a:dual-cohomology"></a>
**Proposition 14**. *The augmentation $Cf\to s$ extends to a projective resolution $R\to s$ with $$R_0=Cf,\qquad R_i=Ce\ (i\ge1),\qquad
d_1=\rho_u,\qquad d_{i+2}=\rho_{\ell_i}\ (i\ge0).
\label{a:simple-resolution}$$ It satisfies $$H^a\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_C(R,s)=
\begin{cases}k&a=0,\\0&a\ne0,\end{cases}
\qquad
H^a\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_C(R,C)=
\begin{cases}Ds&a=2,\\0&a\ne2,\end{cases}
\label{a:dual-cohomology}$$ where the second identification is an isomorphism of right $C$-modules.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-a-resolution"><summary>Proof of stmt-a-resolution</summary>

*Proof.* On the basis $(e,x,y,z,t,j)$ of $Ce$, right multiplication gives $$\begin{aligned}
\rho_u(e,x,y,z,t,j)
  &=(u,v,v,0,(1+q)n,0),\\
\rho_{\ell_i}(e,x,y,z,t,j)
  &=(\ell_i,q^{i+1}z,z,0,(1+q^{i+2})j,0).
\end{aligned}$$ For every $i\ge0$, the coefficient $1+q^{i+2}$ is nonzero. Consequently $$\begin{array}{c|c|c}
\text{map}&\text{image}&\text{kernel}\\ \hline
\rho_u
 &\langle u,v,n\rangle_k
 &\langle\ell_0,z,j\rangle_k\\
\rho_{\ell_i},\ i\ge0
 &\langle\ell_i,z,j\rangle_k
 &\langle\ell_{i+1},z,j\rangle_k.
\end{array}$$ The first image is $\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}(Cf)$, and each later image is the preceding kernel. This proves exactness at every index. Since $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_C(Ce,s)=es=0$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_C(Cf,s)=fs=k$, the first equality in <a href="#a:dual-cohomology" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:dual-cohomology">[a:dual-cohomology]</a> follows.

Under $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_C(Cf,C)=fC$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_C(Ce,C)=eC$, the dual cochain complex is <a id="a:dual-complex"></a>$$fC\xrightarrow{\lambda_u}eC
 \xrightarrow{\lambda_{\ell_0}}eC
 \xrightarrow{\lambda_{\ell_1}}eC
 \xrightarrow{\lambda_{\ell_2}}\cdots,
\label{a:dual-complex}$$ starting in degree zero. The first arrow is injective, with image $\langle u,y+qx,z,v\rangle_k$. For $i\ge0$, $$\lambda_{\ell_i}(e,x,y,z,u,v)
  =(\ell_i,q^iz,qz,0,(1+q^i)v,0).$$ The image and kernel are therefore $$\begin{array}{c|c|c}
 &\text{image}&\text{kernel}\\ \hline
i=0&\langle\ell_0,z\rangle_k
 &\langle\ell_{-1},z,u,v\rangle_k\\
i\ge1&\langle\ell_i,z,v\rangle_k
 &\langle\ell_{i-1},z,v\rangle_k.
\end{array}$$ Since $q\ell_{-1}=qx+y$, the first kernel equals the image of $\lambda_u$. The only remaining cohomology is in degree two: $$\frac{\langle\ell_0,z,v\rangle_k}
     {\langle\ell_0,z\rangle_k}=k\overline v.$$ Finally $vf=v$, $ve=0$, and the only nonzero product of $v$ with a radical basis vector is $vt=qz$, which is a boundary. Thus the radical acts trivially on $k\overline v$, and its right module structure is exactly $Ds$. ◻

</details>

<a id="e:section"></a>
### Polynomial stable endomorphisms and twists

The dual cohomology in Proposition <a href="#a:resolution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:resolution">14</a> produces a degree-three extension class over $T$. We first determine its products, then tensor two copies and use symmetric duality to determine all negative degrees as well.

<a id="stmt-e-polynomial"></a>
<div id="e:polynomial" class="proposition">

<a id="e:triangle"></a>
<a id="e:one-polynomial"></a>
**Proposition 15**. *Put $\mathcal J=T\otimes_C R$, where $R$ is the resolution in <a href="#a:simple-resolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:simple-resolution">[a:simple-resolution]</a>. There is a triangle in the derived category of $T$-modules $$s[2]\longrightarrow\mathcal J\longrightarrow s
 \xrightarrow{\tau}s[3],
\label{e:triangle}$$ and the connecting class generates the Yoneda algebra: $$\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_T^*(s,s)=k[\tau],\qquad |\tau|=3.
\label{e:one-polynomial}$$ The complex $\mathcal J$ is bounded below, has finite projective $T$-terms, and has bounded homology.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-e-polynomial"><summary>Proof of stmt-e-polynomial</summary>

<a id="e:coefficient-sequence"></a>
*Proof.* Tensor the sequence of $(T,C)$-bimodules $0\to DC\to T\to C\to0$ with $R$. Projectivity of each $R_i$ gives a short exact sequence of complexes of left $T$-modules $$0\longrightarrow DC\otimes_C R
 \longrightarrow\mathcal J\longrightarrow R\longrightarrow0.
\label{e:coefficient-sequence}$$ The two outside complexes have action inflated from $C$. For a finite projective left $C$-module $V$, the natural evaluation map $$DC\otimes_C V\longrightarrow D\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_C(V,C),\qquad
\phi\otimes v\longmapsto\bigl(f\mapsto\phi(f(v))\bigr)$$ is an isomorphism, first for a finite free module and then for its summands. Applying this termwise identifies the left complex in <a href="#e:coefficient-sequence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="e:coefficient-sequence">[e:coefficient-sequence]</a> with $D\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_C(R,C)$. By <a href="#a:dual-cohomology" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:dual-cohomology">[a:dual-cohomology]</a>, its only homology is $s$ in homological degree two, with the asserted $C$-module structure. Good truncation therefore identifies it with $s[2]$ in the derived category, also after inflating the action to $T$. Fix this identification. The quotient complex resolves $s$, so <a href="#e:coefficient-sequence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="e:coefficient-sequence">[e:coefficient-sequence]</a> gives <a href="#e:triangle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="e:triangle">[e:triangle]</a>. Its middle complex has projective $T$-terms, and the triangle gives its bounded homology.

<a id="e:polynomial-sequence"></a>
Because $\mathcal J$ is bounded below and projective, ordinary induction–restriction adjunction computes $$Q_i:=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm D^b(T)}(\mathcal J,s[i])
 =H^i\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_T(\mathcal J,s)
 =H^i\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_C(R,s)
 =\begin{cases}k&i=0,\\0&i\ne0.\end{cases}$$ Apply derived Hom into shifts of $s$ to <a href="#e:triangle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="e:triangle">[e:triangle]</a>. With ordinary negative Ext interpreted as zero, the resulting long exact sequence contains $$Q_{i-1}\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_T^{i-3}(s,s)
 \xrightarrow{\alpha\mapsto\alpha[3]\tau}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_T^i(s,s)\longrightarrow Q_i.
\label{e:polynomial-sequence}$$ At $i=1$ this is $k\to0\to\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_T^1(s,s)\to0$, so the first Ext group vanishes. For every $i\ge2$, the middle map in <a href="#e:polynomial-sequence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="e:polynomial-sequence">[e:polynomial-sequence]</a> is an isomorphism. In particular $\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_T^2(s,s)=0$, and at $i=3$ it sends the identity of $s$ to the nonzero class $\tau$. Induction now shows that $\tau^m$ is a basis of $\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_T^{3m}(s,s)$ for every $m\ge0$, and all other positive degrees vanish. Since the isomorphisms are the displayed Yoneda compositions, this proves the algebra assertion <a href="#e:one-polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="e:one-polynomial">[e:one-polynomial]</a>. ◻

</details>

We now tensor two copies of this self-extension algebra. Set <a id="e:stable-groups"></a>$$X=s\otimes s,\qquad
\mathcal H^a=\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_E(X,X[a])\quad(a\in\mathbb Z).
\label{e:stable-groups}$$ Let $\tau_1=\tau\otimes1$ and $\tau_2=1\otimes\tau$ be the two external extension classes. We write $\mathcal P_m$ for the space of polynomials of total exponent $m$ in $\tau_1,\tau_2$; their cohomological degree is $3m$. This notation distinguishes these polynomial spaces from the projective resolutions used later.

<a id="stmt-e-all-degrees"></a>
<div id="e:all-degrees" class="proposition">

<a id="e:two-polynomial"></a>
<a id="e:profile"></a>
**Proposition 16**. *The module $X$ is nonprojective and $$\mathcal H^{\ge0}=k[\tau_1,\tau_2],\qquad |\tau_1|=|\tau_2|=3.
\label{e:two-polynomial}$$ In every integer degree, the only nonzero groups are $$\mathcal H^{3m}=\mathcal P_m,\qquad
\mathcal H^{-3m-1}=D\mathcal P_m\qquad(m\ge0).
\label{e:profile}$$ Under these identifications, postcomposition by a shifted $\tau_i$ between negative groups is the transpose of multiplication by $\tau_i$ on the polynomial spaces. The maps $\mathcal H^{-1}\to\mathcal H^2$ given by either class are zero.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-e-all-degrees"><summary>Proof of stmt-e-all-degrees</summary>

*Proof.* Choose a projective $T$-resolution $Q\to s$. The total complex of $Q\otimes Q$ is a projective $E$-resolution of $X$. There are finitely many summands in each total degree, and termwise finite projectivity gives an isomorphism of cochain complexes $$\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_E(Q\otimes Q,s\otimes s)
 \cong\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_T(Q,s)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_T(Q,s).$$ The cohomology of this tensor product is the tensor product of the cohomologies: over a field each complex splits into its homology and contractible summands. Tensoring comparison maps of resolutions computes the external Yoneda products under this isomorphism. In particular the maps acting on the first and second tensor factors commute, since all interchange signs are $+1$ in characteristic two. Proposition <a href="#e:polynomial" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="e:polynomial">15</a> therefore gives $\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_E^*(X,X)=k[\tau_1,\tau_2]$ as algebras.

Its degree-three part is nonzero, so $X$ is not projective. Also $\mathop{\mathrm{End}}_E(X)=k$, and the identity cannot factor through a projective: such a factorization would exhibit $X$ as a projective summand. Thus $\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{End}}}_E(X)=k$. Positive Ext agrees with positive stable Hom over the symmetric algebra $E$, proving <a href="#e:two-polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="e:two-polynomial">[e:two-polynomial]</a>. Lemma <a href="#h:duality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:duality">5</a> gives $$\mathcal H^{-a-1}\cong D\mathcal H^a\qquad(a\ge0),$$ which proves the support and dimensions in <a href="#e:profile" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="e:profile">[e:profile]</a>.

<a id="e:pairing"></a>
To retain the actual multiplication maps, choose a nonzero functional $\lambda:\mathcal H^{-1}\to k$. The composition pairing from Lemma <a href="#h:duality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:duality">5</a> identifies $\mathcal H^{-3m-1}$ with $D\mathcal P_m$ by $$(\beta,p)_m
 =\lambda\bigl(p[-3m-1]\beta\bigr),
\qquad
\beta\in\mathcal H^{-3m-1},\quad p\in\mathcal P_m.
\label{e:pairing}$$ It is perfect for every $m$. For $m\ge1$, associativity of composition gives, for $p\in\mathcal P_{m-1}$, <a id="e:transpose"></a>$$\bigl(\tau_i[-3m-1]\beta,p\bigr)_{m-1}
 =(\beta,p[3]\tau_i)_m.
\label{e:transpose}$$ The right-hand side is pairing with polynomial multiplication by $\tau_i$. This proves the transpose assertion with its precise shift. For $m=0$, the target is $\mathcal H^2=0$, which proves the boundary assertion as well. ◻

</details>

One can make the negative action especially concrete. Let $\eta_{a,b}\in\mathcal H^{-3(a+b)-1}$ be dual to $\tau_1^a\tau_2^b$. With multiplication here meaning postcomposition by the appropriately shifted class, <a id="e:negative-action"></a>$$\tau_1\eta_{a,b}=
 \begin{cases}\eta_{a-1,b}&a>0,\\0&a=0,\end{cases}
\qquad
\tau_2\eta_{a,b}=
 \begin{cases}\eta_{a,b-1}&b>0,\\0&b=0.\end{cases}
\label{e:negative-action}$$ These formulas include the zero maps out of $\eta_{0,0}$. Products of two negative-degree classes vanish by the support <a href="#e:profile" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="e:profile">[e:profile]</a>.

<a id="e:inverse-restriction"></a>
For $H\in k^\times$, define <a id="e:twist-definition"></a>$$h_H:T\longrightarrow T,\quad (a,\phi)\longmapsto(a,H\phi),
\qquad \sigma_H=h_H\otimes h_H,
\qquad U_H=E_{\sigma_H}.
\label{e:twist-definition}$$ Here $E_{\sigma_H}$ is the regular left module with right action $a\cdot b=a\sigma_H(b)$. These are automorphisms, and $U_H$ is projective on both sides. The tensor functor $U_H(-)$ is restriction by $\sigma_H^{-1}$, as shown by the natural balanced isomorphism $$U_H\otimes_E V\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }
\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{\sigma_H^{-1}}V,\qquad
a\otimes v\longmapsto\sigma_H^{-1}(a)v.
\label{e:inverse-restriction}$$ The same convention applies to $T_{h_H}\otimes_T-$. Let $\chi:E\to k$ be the character of $X$. Since $\chi\sigma_H=\chi$, we fix the resulting identification $U_H X=X$. The exact functor $U_H(-)$ preserves projectives and acts on all groups $\mathcal H^a$, with the shift identifications of Lemma <a href="#h:evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:evaluation">9</a>.

To determine this action in negative degrees, we first choose an explicit generator in degree $-1$. Once the action on this generator is known, the composition pairing determines the action on every negative group.

<a id="stmt-e-socle"></a>
<div id="e:socle" class="lemma">

**Lemma 17**. *Put $$p_f=f\otimes f,\qquad \zeta=f^*\otimes f^*\in E p_f.$$ For every $a,b\in E$, $a\zeta b=\chi(a)\chi(b)\zeta$. The map $i_X:X\to Ep_f$, $i_X(1)=\zeta$, is an embedding with image in $(\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}E)p_f=\Omega X$. Regarded as a map $X\to\Omega X$, it is nonzero stably and spans $\mathcal H^{-1}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-e-socle"><summary>Proof of stmt-e-socle</summary>

*Proof.* The dual basis vector $f^*\in DC$ is the character of the $f$-simple on $C$. The dual bimodule action in <a href="#a:trivial-extension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:trivial-extension">[a:trivial-extension]</a> shows that multiplication on either side by an element of $C$ scales $f^*$ by that character; the square-zero ideal $DC$ kills it on either side. Tensoring proves the asserted action on $\zeta$. The projective cover $Ep_f\to X$, $ap_f\mapsto\chi(a)$, has kernel $(\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}E)p_f$ by <a href="#a:radicals" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:radicals">[a:radicals]</a>. The nonzero vector $\zeta$ lies in this kernel, so the displayed map is an embedding into $\Omega X$.

We verify nonfactorization through every finite projective. Write $J_E=\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}E$. If $b\in E$ satisfies $J_Eb=0$, symmetry gives $$t_E(abj)=t_E(jab)=0\qquad(a\in E,\ j\in J_E),$$ since $ja\in J_E$. Nondegeneracy therefore implies $bJ_E=0$. For any map $X\to E$, every vector in its image satisfies this condition. Any left-module map $E\to J_Ep_f$ has the form $b\mapsto br$ with $r\in J_Ep_f$, so its composite with $X\to E$ is zero. The same conclusion holds for finite sums and summands of $E$. Thus all maps $X\to\Omega X$ factoring through a finite projective are zero, whereas $i_X\ne0$. Finally $\mathcal H^{-1}=D\mathcal P_0$ is one-dimensional by Proposition <a href="#e:all-degrees" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="e:all-degrees">16</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-e-twists"></a>
<div id="e:twists" class="proposition">

<a id="e:twist-weights"></a>
**Proposition 18**. *For every $H\in k^\times$ and every $m\ge0$, the action of $U_H(-)$ on the groups in <a href="#e:profile" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="e:profile">[e:profile]</a> is $$\left.U_H\right|_{\mathcal H^{3m}}=H^{-m}\,1_{\mathcal P_m},
\qquad
\left.U_H\right|_{\mathcal H^{-3m-1}}=H^{m+2}\,1_{D\mathcal P_m}.
\label{e:twist-weights}$$ In particular, the negative action is the stated scalar on the entire $(m+1)$-dimensional space.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-e-twists"><summary>Proof of stmt-e-twists</summary>

*Proof.* First twist <a href="#e:coefficient-sequence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="e:coefficient-sequence">[e:coefficient-sequence]</a> by restriction along $h_H^{-1}$. Because $h_H$ fixes $C$, the map $h_H\otimes1$ identifies its middle complex with the original one. It induces the identity on the quotient $R$ and multiplication by $H$ on $DC\otimes_C R$. Write $\tau_H$ for the twist of $\tau$. Passing to the fixed derived identifications yields a commutative diagram of triangles $$\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large]
s[2]\arrow[r]\arrow[d,"H"']&
\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{h_H^{-1}}\mathcal J\arrow[r]
 \arrow[d,"h_H\otimes1"]&
s\arrow[r,"\tau_H"]\arrow[d,"1"]&
s[3]\arrow[d,"H"]\\
s[2]\arrow[r]&\mathcal J\arrow[r]&s\arrow[r,"\tau"]&s[3].
\end{tikzcd}$$ Naturality of the connecting morphism gives $H\tau_H=\tau$. Hence each external class $\tau_i$ is sent to $H^{-1}\tau_i$, and preservation of products proves the first formula in <a href="#e:twist-weights" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="e:twist-weights">[e:twist-weights]</a>.

For the negative part we first compute the action on $\mathcal H^{-1}$. Twist the projective presentation $$0\longrightarrow\Omega X\longrightarrow Ep_f
 \longrightarrow X\longrightarrow0.$$ Under <a href="#e:inverse-restriction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="e:inverse-restriction">[e:inverse-restriction]</a>, its comparison with the original presentation is $$\sigma_H:\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{\sigma_H^{-1}}(Ep_f)\longrightarrow Ep_f.$$ It is $E$-linear, since $\sigma_H(\sigma_H^{-1}(a)b)=a\sigma_H(b)$, and induces the identity on $X$. On the kernels it sends $\zeta$ to $H^2\zeta$. Thus the generator in Lemma <a href="#e:socle" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="e:socle">17</a> is sent to $H^2$ times itself, proving that $U_H$ acts by $H^2$ on $\mathcal H^{-1}$.

Finally the exact twist functor preserves shifted composition. Applying it to the product in <a href="#e:pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="e:pairing">[e:pairing]</a>, and using its actions on $\mathcal P_m$ and $\mathcal H^{-1}$, gives $$\bigl(U_H(\beta),H^{-m}p\bigr)_m
 =H^2(\beta,p)_m
\qquad
(\beta\in\mathcal H^{-3m-1},\ p\in\mathcal P_m).$$ Equivalently, $(U_H(\beta),p)_m=H^{m+2}(\beta,p)_m$ for every $p$. Nondegeneracy of the pairing forces $U_H(\beta)=H^{m+2}\beta$ for every $\beta$, as asserted. ◻

</details>

<a id="b:section"></a>
### Derived induction and the trace obstruction

We now construct the bimodule that will join the two twists in the triangular algebra. The two-factor filtration follows the organization of (OpenAI 2026b, sec. 6) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[OpenAIAR2026](#ref-OpenAIAR2026)]. A related use of tensor products of bimodules attached to Hochschild classes occurs in support-variety theory (Erdmann et al. 2003, Definition 2.1 and Theorem 2.3) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[ErdmannEtAl2003](#ref-ErdmannEtAl2003)]. Here the bimodule comes from derived induction, and its evaluated lifting property is proved by a trace obstruction.

<a id="stmt-b-bimodule"></a>
<div id="b:bimodule" class="proposition">

<a id="b:W"></a>
**Proposition 19**. *There is a finite $E$-bimodule $Y$, projective separately as a left and as a right $E$-module, such that $$\label{b:W}
 W^a:=\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_E(X,(YX)[a])
 \cong
 \begin{cases}
 k,&a=-3,0,\\
 0,&a\notin\{-3,0\}.
 \end{cases}$$ For every $H\in k^\times$, there is an ordinary bimodule map $g_H:U_H\to Y$ whose evaluation, under $U_HX=X$, represents a nonzero element of $W^0$. The same $Y$ works for all $H$, and all modules and maps are defined over $k$.*

</div>

The two assertions require different arguments. A filtration of derived induction will compute the groups $W^a$. In particular, $W^0=k$ provides nonzero stable $E$-module maps $X\to YX$, but does not show that one is the evaluation of a bimodule map $U_H\to Y$. The trace obstruction, combined with stable duality, will prove that this evaluation has nonzero image for every $H$.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### A finite representative of derived induction

Let $K$ be the two-sided bar complex for derived induction: $$K_i=E\otimes B^{\otimes i}\otimes E\quad(i\geq0),\qquad K_i=0\quad(i<0).$$ This is the ordinary-algebra instance of the bar model for derived tensor functors; see (Keller 1994, sec. 6.1 and 6.6) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[Keller1994](#ref-Keller1994)]. The finite representative needed here will follow from the separate projective-dimension bounds on the two sides. For $i\geq1$, its differential is $$\begin{aligned}
 d_i(a\otimes b_1\otimes\cdots\otimes b_i\otimes c)
 ={}&
 ab_1\otimes b_2\otimes\cdots\otimes b_i\otimes c\\
 &+\sum_{r=1}^{i-1}
 a\otimes b_1\otimes\cdots\otimes b_rb_{r+1}
       \otimes\cdots\otimes b_i\otimes c\\
 &+a\otimes b_1\otimes\cdots\otimes b_{i-1}\otimes b_ic .
\end{aligned}$$ As throughout, signs disappear in characteristic two. Associativity gives $d_{i-1}d_i=0$, since each double multiplication occurs twice. Multiplication in degree zero defines a chain map $m:K\to E$.

<a id="stmt-b-bar-model"></a>
<div id="b:bar-model" class="lemma">

**Lemma 20**. *The complex $K$ represents $E\otimes_B^{\mathbf L}E$, has finite projective $E^e$-terms and has homology only in degrees $0,\ldots,4$. The bimodule $c_5K$ is projective on both sides. Consequently its stable image $$\mathcal I=\langle K\rangle=(c_5K)[5]$$ has finite side-projective representatives, as does $\mathcal I[-1]$. One such representative $Y$ of $\mathcal I[-1]$ is obtained by taking four successive finite bimodule cosyzygies of $c_5K$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-b-bar-model"><summary>Proof of stmt-b-bar-model</summary>

*Proof.* The usual bar resolution of $B$ has terms $B\otimes B^{\otimes i}\otimes B$. Its augmented complex is contracted as a complex of vector spaces by inserting a unit. Inserting a unit at one end also proves that tensoring it with a module at the other end gives a projective resolution of that module on the remaining side. Inducing the two outer actions gives $K$, with each term a finite free $E^e$-module.

On the left, $B\otimes B^{\otimes i}\otimes E$ is a projective $B$-resolution of ${}_BE$. By Proposition <a href="#a:dual-dimensions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:dual-dimensions">13</a>, this module has a projective resolution supported in degrees $0,\ldots,4$. Comparison of projective resolutions gives a homotopy equivalence between these two resolutions. Applying $E\otimes_B-$ preserves the homotopy equivalence, so $K$, as a left $E$-complex, is homotopy equivalent to a complex of projectives supported in those degrees. The same argument using $E_B$ works on the right. In particular $H_i(K)=0$ for $i>4$.

For completeness, this equivalence proves projectivity of the specific cokernel $c_5K$. On either side, choose a homotopy from the identity of $K$ to a map factoring through the complex ending in degree $4$. In degree $5$ it gives $$1_{K_5}=d_6h_5+h_4d_5.$$ Modulo $\mathop{\mathrm{im}}d_6$, the identity of $c_5K$ therefore factors as $$c_5K\xrightarrow{\ \overline d_5\ }K_4
       \xrightarrow{\ q_5h_4\ }c_5K,$$ where $q_5:K_5\to c_5K$ is the quotient map. Thus $c_5K$ is a summand of a projective on each side. This assertion concerns the two side-module structures; the two splittings need not be bimodule splittings. The high exact sequences $0\to c_{n+1}K\to K_n\to c_nK\to0$, for $n\geq5$, now split on each side. Induction shows that all higher cokernels are side-projective as well.

To make the finite choice explicit, put $N_0=c_5K$, and for $r=0,1,2,3$ use $$0\longrightarrow N_r\longrightarrow
 \mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_k(E^e,N_r)\longrightarrow N_{r+1}\longrightarrow0,\qquad
 n\longmapsto(a\longmapsto an).$$ Here the $E^e$-action on the middle term is by right multiplication on its input. The middle term is finite injective, hence projective because $E^e$ is symmetric. Since $N_r$ is projective and therefore injective on each $E$-side, this sequence splits on both sides; it follows inductively that every $N_r$ is side-projective. Take $Y=N_4$. Its stable class is $(c_5K)[4]=\mathcal I[-1]$, as required. Lemmas <a href="#h:stable-image" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:stable-image">7</a> and <a href="#h:evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:evaluation">9</a> give the stated stable images and their evaluation compatibilities. We denote the stable map induced by $m$ by $\overline m:\mathcal I\to E$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### The stable Hom profile in every degree

<a id="stmt-b-profile"></a>
<div id="b:profile" class="lemma">

**Lemma 21**. *The bimodule $Y$ of Lemma <a href="#b:bar-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="b:bar-model">20</a> satisfies <a href="#b:W" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="b:W">[b:W]</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-b-profile"><summary>Proof of stmt-b-profile</summary>

*Proof.* The evaluated complex is $$K\otimes_E X=E\otimes B^{\otimes\bullet}\otimes X.$$ Before inducing to $E$, its bar model and $R\otimes R$ are projective $B$-resolutions of $X=s\otimes s$. They are homotopy equivalent. Induction preserves this equivalence and gives <a id="b:evaluated-model"></a>$$\label{b:evaluated-model}
 K\otimes_E X\simeq
 (T\otimes_C R)\otimes(T\otimes_C R)
 =\mathcal J\otimes\mathcal J .$$ In particular, Lemma <a href="#h:evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:evaluation">9</a> identifies its stable image with $\mathcal IX$.

We retain the actual attaching maps in the triangle $$s[2]\longrightarrow\mathcal J\longrightarrow s
       \xrightarrow{\tau}s[3]$$ of <a href="#e:triangle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="e:triangle">[e:triangle]</a>. Choose bounded-below projective models $A_\bullet$ for $s[2]$ and $Q_\bullet$ for $s$, and a chain map $t:Q_\bullet\to A_\bullet[1]$ representing $\tau$. The cone of $t[-1]$ has underlying graded complex $A_\bullet\oplus Q_\bullet$, differential $$\begin{pmatrix}d_A&t\\0&d_Q\end{pmatrix},$$ and represents $\mathcal J$, by Lemma <a href="#h:derived-models" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:derived-models">6</a>. Indeed the cone construction for the rotated triangle gives this identification; comparisons of projective models lift the connecting map and give homotopy equivalences of the cones. Thus this is a homotopy equivalent projective model, with its specified subcomplex and quotient, rather than only an equality of homology groups.

<a id="b:three-levels"></a>
Tensor two copies of this model and shift by $[-4]$. Filter first by $A_\bullet\otimes A_\bullet$, then add $Q_\bullet\otimes A_\bullet$ and $A_\bullet\otimes Q_\bullet$, and finally add $Q_\bullet\otimes Q_\bullet$. Every inclusion is split in each graded degree, and the successive quotients resolve $$\label{b:three-levels}
 X,\qquad X[-2]\oplus X[-2],\qquad X[-4].$$ The stable image of the full complex is $\mathcal L=(\mathcal IX)[-4]$. With the middle terms in the displayed order, the adjacent connecting maps are the shifted row and column $$(\tau_1,\tau_2),\qquad
  \binom{\tau_2}{\tau_1}.$$ These are the cross differentials $t$ in the relevant factor and the identity in the other factor. Lemma <a href="#h:stable-image" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:stable-image">7</a> identifies them with the connecting maps after taking stable images.

We first compute the kernels and cokernels of these adjacent maps in every degree. On the polynomial groups the maps form the two-variable Koszul sequence; on the negative groups they form its dual. We then use the two long exact sequences of the filtration to control the full top attaching map, including its possible component into the bottom level.

<a id="b:koszul-middle"></a>
<a id="b:koszul-ends"></a>
Write $\mathcal P_m=k[\tau_1,\tau_2]_m$ for polynomials of total exponent $m$, and put $\mathcal P_m=0$ for $m<0$. By Proposition <a href="#e:all-degrees" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="e:all-degrees">16</a>, $$\mathcal H^{3m}=\mathcal P_m,\qquad
 \mathcal H^{-3m-1}=D\mathcal P_m\quad(m\geq0),$$ and all other $\mathcal H^a$ vanish. Multiplication by a degree-three class on the negative groups is transpose to polynomial multiplication. For each integer $l$, denote the adjacent maps by $$\begin{aligned}
 d_l:(\mathcal H^{l-2})^2&\longrightarrow\mathcal H^{l+1},&
 d_l(u,v)&=\tau_1u+\tau_2v,\\
 d'_l:\mathcal H^{l-4}&\longrightarrow(\mathcal H^{l-1})^2,&
 d'_l(w)&=(\tau_2w,\tau_1w).
 \end{aligned}$$ Products in these formulas mean shifted postcomposition. We claim $$\begin{aligned}
 \ker d_l&=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}d'_{l-1},\label{b:koszul-middle}\\
 \mathop{\mathrm{coker}}d_{l-1}&=
 \begin{cases}k,&l=0,\\0,&l\ne0,\end{cases}
 \qquad
 \ker d'_l=
 \begin{cases}k,&l=3,\\0,&l\ne3.\end{cases}
 \label{b:koszul-ends}
\end{aligned}$$

<a id="b:polynomial-koszul"></a>
To check the middle identity, the polynomial calculation for $m\geq0$ is the exact sequence $$\label{b:polynomial-koszul}
 0\longrightarrow\mathcal P_{m-1}
 \xrightarrow{\,(\tau_2,\tau_1)^{\mathsf t}\,}
 \mathcal P_m^2
 \xrightarrow{\,(\tau_1,\tau_2)\,}
 \mathcal P_{m+1}\longrightarrow0 .$$ The row is onto since every positive-degree monomial is divisible by a variable. If $\tau_1u+\tau_2v=0$, coprimality implies $u=\tau_2w$ and $v=\tau_1w$, uniquely; the signs agree in characteristic two. When the middle group is $(D\mathcal P_m)^2$, use the dual of <a href="#b:polynomial-koszul" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="b:polynomial-koszul">[b:polynomial-koszul]</a>, interchanging the two middle slots: $$D\mathcal P_{m+1}\longrightarrow
 (D\mathcal P_m)^2\longrightarrow D\mathcal P_{m-1}.$$ At $m=0$, the last term is the actual group $\mathcal H^2=0$, and the first map $D\mathcal P_1\to(D\mathcal P_0)^2$ is an isomorphism. This is the seam $l=1$. If the middle group is unsupported, both its kernel and the image into it are zero. This proves <a href="#b:koszul-middle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="b:koszul-middle">[b:koszul-middle]</a> for every $l$.

For the row cokernel, a target $\mathcal H^l=\mathcal P_m$ gives $\mathcal P_{m-1}^2\to\mathcal P_m$, which is onto for $m\geq1$ and has cokernel $k$ for $m=0$. A negative target $\mathcal H^l=D\mathcal P_m$ gives the dual of the injective column $\mathcal P_m\to\mathcal P_{m+1}^2$, and is onto for every $m\geq0$. All other targets are zero. For the column kernel, a nonnegative source $\mathcal P_m$ maps injectively into $\mathcal P_{m+1}^2$. A source $D\mathcal P_m$ for $m\geq1$ is the dual of the surjective row $\mathcal P_{m-1}^2\to\mathcal P_m$, so again the column is injective. The remaining source is $\mathcal H^{-1}=D\mathcal P_0=k$, at $l=3$, with zero target $(\mathcal H^2)^2$. These cases prove <a href="#b:koszul-ends" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="b:koszul-ends">[b:koszul-ends]</a>. They include the only possible multiplication from a supported negative degree across zero, namely $\mathcal H^{-1}\to\mathcal H^2=0$.

<a id="b:first-two-levels"></a>
It remains to pass from adjacent maps to the whole filtered object. Let $\mathcal L_1$ be the stable image of the lower two levels of <a href="#b:three-levels" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="b:three-levels">[b:three-levels]</a>, and put $N^l=\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_E(X,\mathcal L_1[l])$. The first two levels give $$\label{b:first-two-levels}
 0\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{coker}}d_{l-1}\longrightarrow N^l
 \xrightarrow{q_l}\ker d_l\longrightarrow0.$$ Let $$r_l:\mathcal H^{l-4}\longrightarrow N^{l+1}$$ be the *actual* connecting map obtained by attaching the top level. Naturality of the quotient to the middle level gives $q_{l+1}r_l=d'_l$. Thus $\ker r_l\subseteq\ker d'_l$, which is zero unless $l=3$. At that index $N^4=0$: its left term in <a href="#b:first-two-levels" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="b:first-two-levels">[b:first-two-levels]</a> is $\mathop{\mathrm{coker}}d_3=0$, and its right term is a kernel in $(\mathcal H^2)^2=0$. Hence $\ker r_3=\mathcal H^{-1}=k$.

Moreover, $$q_l(\mathop{\mathrm{im}}r_{l-1})=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}d'_{l-1}=\ker d_l.$$ For $l\ne0$, $q_l$ is an isomorphism, so $r_{l-1}$ is onto. At $l=0$, the vanishing $\mathcal H^{-2}=0$ gives $N^0=k$, while the whole incoming source of $r_{-1}$ is $\mathcal H^{-5}=0$. Consequently $\mathop{\mathrm{coker}}r_{l-1}$ is $k$ at $l=0$ and zero elsewhere.

The final long exact sequence gives $$0\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{coker}}r_{l-1}
 \longrightarrow\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_E(X,\mathcal L[l])
 \longrightarrow\ker r_l\longrightarrow0.$$ The two scalar groups therefore survive precisely at $l=0,3$. This argument permits an additional component of the top attaching map into the bottom level: $N^4=0$ and $\mathcal H^{-5}=0$ already force the asserted kernel and cokernel for the full $r_l$.
