Finally $YX=(\mathcal IX)[-1]=\mathcal L[3]$, so $l=a+3$. This gives exactly <a href="#b:W" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="b:W">[b:W]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### From perfect factorizations to an ordinary trace

The required Hom profile is now established. It remains to construct bimodule maps from the twists whose evaluations generate its degree-zero line. We first show that a particular character map cannot factor through a perfect complex; stable duality will then turn this obstruction into the required maps.

Let $\chi:E\to k$ be the character of $X$, write $\chi_B=\chi|_B$, and retain $p_f=f\otimes f$. The $B$-bimodule structure on $DB$ is $(a\lambda b)(c)=\lambda(bca)$.

We use the bimodule $DB\otimes B$, with its outer actions, to connect perfect factorizations with ordinary traces. Using the bound $\mathop{\mathrm{id}}_B B\leq4$, left coinduction will replace a perfect complex by a bounded complex with terms in $\mathop{\mathrm{add}}(DB\otimes B)$. For an ordinary composite $v:B\to DB$ through $DB\otimes B$, the identification $DB\otimes B\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_k(B,B)$ will express the scalar $v(1)(p_f)$ as the trace of a linear operator.

<a id="stmt-b-ordinary-factorization"></a>
<div id="b:ordinary-factorization" class="lemma">

**Lemma 22**. *If a bimodule map $v:B\to DB$ factors in the derived category through a perfect $B^e$-complex, then it factors by ordinary bimodule maps through an object of $\mathop{\mathrm{add}}(DB\otimes B)$, where the tensor product has its outer bimodule actions.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-b-ordinary-factorization"><summary>Proof of stmt-b-ordinary-factorization</summary>

*Proof.* Represent the perfect intermediate object by a bounded complex $P$ of finite projective bimodules. We first replace it by a bounded complex $P'$ whose terms belong to $\mathop{\mathrm{add}}(DB\otimes B)$.

<a id="b:coresolution"></a>
For a finite bimodule $L$, define $$J_B(L)=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_k(B,L),\qquad
 (a\phi b)(c)=\phi(ca)b,\qquad
 j_L(z)(c)=cz.$$ The map $j_L:L\to J_B(L)$ is a natural bimodule monomorphism. Set $C^0(L)=L$ and $C^{r+1}(L)=\mathop{\mathrm{coker}}j_{C^r(L)}$. These are additive functors and preserve split retracts. For a fixed $d=4$ we have the functorial exact sequence $$\label{b:coresolution}
 0\longrightarrow L\longrightarrow J_B(C^0(L))
 \longrightarrow\cdots\longrightarrow J_B(C^{d-1}(L))
 \longrightarrow C^d(L)\longrightarrow0.$$

For the free bimodule $L=B\otimes B$, this construction is the left-module coinduced coresolution of $B$, tensored over $k$ with the right regular module $B$. This follows from $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_k(B,B\otimes B)=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_k(B,B)\otimes B$ and from exactness of tensoring vector spaces. Each coinduced left-module term is a finite injective, hence belongs to $\mathop{\mathrm{add}}(DB)$. The last left-module cokernel is also injective: by Proposition <a href="#a:dual-dimensions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:dual-dimensions">13</a>, $\mathop{\mathrm{id}}({}_BB)\leq4$, and dimension shifting gives, for every finite left module $V$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_B^1(V,C^d(B))
 \cong\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_B^{d+1}(V,B)=0.$$ Here $C^d(B)$ denotes the same coresolution construction on the left regular module. Thus every term on the right of $L$ in <a href="#b:coresolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="b:coresolution">[b:coresolution]</a>, including the last cokernel, lies in $\mathop{\mathrm{add}}(DB\otimes B)$ when $L=B\otimes B$. The assertion extends to every finite projective bimodule $L$, since the functors in <a href="#b:coresolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="b:coresolution">[b:coresolution]</a> preserve finite sums and split retracts.

Apply this one fixed finite coresolution to each term of $P$. Naturality provides the horizontal maps, giving a bounded double complex. Put the first coinduced term in homological degree $0$ relative to $L$, and the last cokernel in degree $-d$. Its totalization $P'$ is bounded and has all terms in $\mathop{\mathrm{add}}(DB\otimes B)$. The augmentation $P\to P'$ is a quasi-isomorphism: filtering its cone by the finitely many original degrees has exact associated complexes <a href="#b:coresolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="b:coresolution">[b:coresolution]</a>. Thus the given derived factorization can be written $B\to P'\to DB$.

<a id="b:two-orthogonalities"></a>
For $V\in\mathop{\mathrm{add}}(DB\otimes B)$ and $i>0$, we next prove $$\label{b:two-orthogonalities}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_{B^e}^i(B,V)=0,\qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_{B^e}^i(V,DB)=0.$$ It suffices to take $V=DB\otimes B$. Coinduction identifies, for a bimodule $Q$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{B^e}(Q,DB\otimes B)
 \cong\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{B^{\mathrm{op}}}(\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}Q,B).$$ A projective bimodule resolution of $B$ restricts to a right-projective resolution of the free right module $B$. This proves the first vanishing. For the second, tensor a left-projective resolution $Q_\bullet$ of $DB$ over $k$ with the right regular module $B$. It is a projective bimodule resolution of $DB\otimes B$, and $$\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{B^e}(Q_\bullet\otimes B,DB)
 \cong\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_B(Q_\bullet,DB).$$ Its positive cohomology vanishes since ${}_BDB$ is injective. Both assertions pass to finite sums and summands.

<a id="b:Hom-comparisons"></a>
Finally choose a projective resolution $Q_B\to B$ and an injective coresolution $DB\to I_{DB}$, regarded as homological complexes. There are natural quasi-isomorphisms of Hom complexes $$\label{b:Hom-comparisons}
 \begin{split}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}^\bullet_{B^e}(B,P')&\longrightarrow
       \mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}^\bullet_{B^e}(Q_B,P'),\\
 \mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}^\bullet_{B^e}(P',DB)&\longrightarrow
       \mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}^\bullet_{B^e}(P',I_{DB}).
 \end{split}$$ To verify them, filter by the finitely many terms of $P'$. For each successive quotient the claim is one of <a href="#b:two-orthogonalities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="b:two-orthogonalities">[b:two-orthogonalities]</a>, together with equality in degree zero and vanishing of negative Ext between modules. A shift of a term merely shifts this comparison. Hence terms in negative degrees cause no exception, and induction on this finite filtration proves <a href="#b:Hom-comparisons" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="b:Hom-comparisons">[b:Hom-comparisons]</a>.

Taking degree-zero cohomology shows that both derived arrows have actual chain-map representatives $\alpha:B[0]\to P'$ and $\beta:P'\to DB[0]$. Their composite is the ordinary map $\beta_0\alpha_0$, so it factors through $P'_0\in\mathop{\mathrm{add}}(DB\otimes B)$. It equals $v$ as an ordinary bimodule map, because $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm D(B^e)}(B,DB)=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{B^e}(B,DB)$. ◻

</details>

The scalar trace calculation below is related to the trace and corner-dimension calculation in (Liu, Zhou, and Zimmermann 2012, Example 3.2(1) and proof of Theorem 3.8) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[LiuZhouZimmermann2012](#ref-LiuZhouZimmermann2012)]. Here the preceding lemma supplies the reduction from perfect factorizations, and the calculation uses the split corner table <a href="#a:corner-parity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:corner-parity">[a:corner-parity]</a>.

<a id="stmt-b-trace-obstruction"></a>
<div id="b:trace-obstruction" class="lemma">

<a id="b:character-map"></a>
**Lemma 23**. *The bimodule map $$\label{b:character-map}
 \eta:B\longrightarrow DB,\qquad
 \eta(a)=\chi_B(a)\chi_B,$$ does not factor in the derived category through a perfect $B^e$-complex.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-b-trace-obstruction"><summary>Proof of stmt-b-trace-obstruction</summary>

*Proof.* The character identities make $\eta$ a bimodule map. By Lemma <a href="#b:ordinary-factorization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="b:ordinary-factorization">22</a>, a perfect factorization would give an ordinary factorization through $\mathop{\mathrm{add}}(DB\otimes B)$. We show that every such factorization $B\to DB$ sends $1$ to a functional vanishing on $p_f$.

First consider maps $$B\xrightarrow{\alpha}DB\otimes B\xrightarrow{\beta}DB.$$ Identify $DB\otimes B$ with $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_k(B,B)$ by $\lambda\otimes c\mapsto[x\mapsto\lambda(x)c]$. The element $\alpha(1)$ satisfies $a\alpha(1)=\alpha(1)a$. Under this identification, its corresponding linear map $f_0$ therefore satisfies $$f_0(xa)=f_0(x)a\quad(x,a\in B).$$ Thus $f_0(x)=d_0x$, where $d_0=f_0(1)$. If $(c_i)$ is any vector-space basis of $B$, with dual basis $(c_i^*)$, then $$\alpha(1)=\sum_i c_i^*\otimes d_0c_i.$$ Right linearity of $\beta$ reduces it to the left-module endomorphism $\lambda\mapsto\beta(\lambda\otimes1)$ of $DB$. Duality identifies these endomorphisms with endomorphisms of the right regular module $B$, which are left multiplications. Thus the endomorphism of $DB$ is right multiplication by some $b'\in B$. Consequently $$\beta(\lambda\otimes c)=\lambda b'c,\qquad
 (\beta\alpha(1))(p_f)
   =\sum_i c_i^*(b'd_0c_ip_f)
   =\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_k\bigl(L_{b'd_0}R_{p_f}\bigr).$$ Here $L_a$ and $R_a$ are left and right multiplication on the vector space $B$. The order $b'd_0$ follows from the dual action $(\lambda b'c)(p_f)=\lambda(b'cp_f)$.

Write $b'd_0=r+\sum_v a_vp_v$, where $r\in\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}B$ and the $p_v$ are its four vertex idempotents. Left and right multiplications commute, and $p_f^2=p_f$, so $$(L_rR_{p_f})^n=L_{r^n}R_{p_f}=0$$ for sufficiently large $n$. The radical part has trace zero. For each $v$, the operator $L_{p_v}R_{p_f}$ is a projection onto $p_vBp_f$. The corner dimensions of $B$, obtained from the corner table <a href="#a:corner-parity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:corner-parity">[a:corner-parity]</a>, are even; in particular $\dim_k(p_vBp_f)=4$ for every $v$. Each projection therefore has trace zero in characteristic two. Linearity gives $(\beta\alpha(1))(p_f)=0$.

A factorization through a finite sum of $DB\otimes B$ is a sum of such composites. A factorization through a summand extends to that finite sum by its split inclusion and projection. The same vanishing thus holds throughout $\mathop{\mathrm{add}}(DB\otimes B)$. But $\eta(1)(p_f)=\chi_B(p_f)=1$, a contradiction. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Lifts with nonzero evaluation

We now apply the trace obstruction to obtain the maps required by Proposition <a href="#b:bimodule" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="b:bimodule">19</a>.

Put $S=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_k(X,X)$, with bimodule action $$(atb)(x)=a\,t(bx).$$ The representation gives a bimodule map $\pi:E\to S$. Since $X$ is one-dimensional, $S=k$ with both actions given by $\chi$.

<a id="stmt-b-stable-adjunction"></a>
<div id="b:stable-adjunction" class="lemma">

**Lemma 24**. *For every finite side-projective bimodule $L$, ordinary tensor–Hom adjunction induces a natural isomorphism $$\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_{E^e}(L,S)\ \cong\ \underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_E(LX,X).$$ This assertion does not require $S$ to be side-projective.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-b-stable-adjunction"><summary>Proof of stmt-b-stable-adjunction</summary>

*Proof.* The ordinary adjoint of $f:L\to S$ is $\widehat f(l\otimes x)=f(l)(x)$. A factorization of $f$ through a finite $E^e$-projective evaluates to a factorization through an $E$-projective.

Conversely embed $L$ in a finite projective-injective $E^e$-module $J$. The embedding splits as a right $E$-module map because $L_E$ is projective, hence injective over symmetric $E$. Thus $LX\to JX$ is injective. Suppose $\widehat f$ factors as $LX\to P\to X$ with $P$ finite $E$-projective. Injectivity of $P$ extends its first factor to $JX\to P$. Composing with $P\to X$ and applying ordinary adjunction gives $J\to S$ whose restriction to $L$ is $f$. This is a bimodule projective factorization. The two factorization ideals therefore correspond exactly. Naturality is inherited from ordinary adjunction. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-b-evaluated-lift"></a>
<div id="b:evaluated-lift" class="lemma">

**Lemma 25**. *For every $H\in k^\times$, there is a stable bimodule map $U_H[1]\to\mathcal I$ with nonzero evaluated stable map $X[1]\to\mathcal IX$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-b-evaluated-lift"><summary>Proof of stmt-b-evaluated-lift</summary>

<a id="b:transpose"></a>
*Proof.* Write $U=U_H$ and $\phi=\pi\overline m:\mathcal I\to S$. All shifts of $U$ and $\mathcal I$ have finite side-projective representatives by Lemma <a href="#h:evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:evaluation">9</a>. Consider the finite-dimensional linear map $$\phi_*:\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_{E^e}(U[1],\mathcal I)
 \longrightarrow\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_{E^e}(U[1],S).$$ Symmetric duality, Lemma <a href="#h:duality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:duality">5</a> applied over $E^e$, identifies its transpose with $$\label{b:transpose}
 \underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_{E^e}(S,U)\longrightarrow\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_{E^e}(\mathcal I,U),
 \qquad b\longmapsto b\phi.$$ Indeed the fixed first argument is $U[1]$, and its duality shift is $U[1][-1]=U$; naturality in the second argument gives the displayed precomposition.

Let $\zeta=f^*\otimes f^*\in E$, as in Lemma <a href="#e:socle" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="e:socle">17</a>. Both its left and right actions are the character $\chi$. The map $$b_0:S\longrightarrow U,\qquad b_0(1)=\zeta$$ is a bimodule map even for the twisted right action, because $$\zeta\cdot b=\zeta\sigma_H(b)
       =\chi(\sigma_H(b))\zeta=\chi(b)\zeta.$$ We will prove that $b_0\phi\ne0$ stably.

Suppose otherwise. The derived map $b_0\pi m:K\to U$ then has zero stable image and factors through a perfect $E^e$-complex by Lemma <a href="#h:perfect-factor" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:perfect-factor">8</a>. To apply that lemma with projective models, one may take a bounded-below projective resolution $Q\to U$ with finite terms and lift the global chain map $K\to U$ to $K\to Q$; its stable image is the same map $b_0\phi$.

Restriction of this factorization to $B^e$ still has a perfect intermediate object. Indeed an $E^e$-free module restricts to the outer bimodule $E\otimes E$. Tensoring the left and right $B$-projective resolutions of $E$ from Proposition <a href="#a:dual-dimensions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:dual-dimensions">13</a> gives a $B^e$-projective resolution of length at most $8$. Finite sums and summands have the same bound. A bounded complex of these restricted modules is perfect by its finite term filtration.

Let $\operatorname{Bar}(B)\to B$ denote the bar resolution. Inclusion of both outer $B$-factors in $E$ defines a $B^e$-chain map $$\iota:\operatorname{Bar}(B)\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{B^e}K,$$ and hence a derived map $B\to\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{B^e}K$. Since $\sigma_H$ fixes $B$, the decomposition $$\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{B^e}U
   =B\oplus(DC\otimes C)\oplus(C\otimes DC)\oplus DB$$ is a decomposition of $B$-bimodules. Let $\rho$ be projection to the last summand. The composite $$\operatorname{Bar}(B)\xrightarrow{\iota}\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}K
 \xrightarrow{\,b_0\pi m\,}\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}U\xrightarrow{\rho}DB$$ is exactly the character map $\eta$ of <a href="#b:character-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="b:character-map">[b:character-map]</a> composed with the bar augmentation: in degree zero it sends $a\otimes c$ to $\chi_B(ac)\chi_B$, since $\rho(\zeta)=\chi_B$, and in higher degrees it is zero. Thus our supposed factorization would make $\eta$ factor through a perfect $B^e$-complex. Lemma <a href="#b:trace-obstruction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="b:trace-obstruction">23</a> excludes this possibility. We have proved $b_0\phi\ne0$.

It follows from <a href="#b:transpose" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="b:transpose">[b:transpose]</a> that $\phi_*$ is nonzero. Choose $\beta:U[1]\to\mathcal I$ with $\phi\beta\ne0$. Lemma <a href="#b:stable-adjunction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="b:stable-adjunction">24</a>, applied only to the side-projective source $U[1]$, identifies this nonzero class with the composite $$U[1]X\xrightarrow{\beta X}\mathcal IX
       \xrightarrow{\overline m X}EX\cong X .$$ This identity follows from ordinary adjunction and from the evaluation of the representation $\pi$. The evaluated maps involving $U[1],\mathcal I,E$ are defined stably by Lemma <a href="#h:evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:evaluation">9</a>; no stable evaluation functor on the nonside-projective module $S$ is needed. The composite is nonzero, so $\beta X$ itself is nonzero. The canonical shift comparison identifies $U[1]X$ with $(UX)[1]=X[1]$, proving the assertion. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-b-evaluated-lift-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-b-evaluated-lift</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#b:bimodule" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="b:bimodule">19</a>.* Take the fixed finite side-projective $Y$ constructed in Lemma <a href="#b:bar-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="b:bar-model">20</a>. Lemma <a href="#b:profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="b:profile">21</a> proves its required Hom profile. For any $H\in k^\times$, shift the map of Lemma <a href="#b:evaluated-lift" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="b:evaluated-lift">25</a> by $[-1]$ and identify $\mathcal I[-1]$ with $Y$ to obtain a stable map $U_H\to Y$. By the shift and evaluation compatibilities of Lemma <a href="#h:evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:evaluation">9</a>, its evaluation is a nonzero class $X\to YX$. A stable map between the fixed finite modules is represented by an ordinary bimodule map; choose one and call it $g_H$. All Hom spaces, dualities, and selections used above are over the original field $k$, so this requires no scalar extension. The construction of $Y$ did not depend on $H$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:triangular"></a>
### The triangular module and the counterexample

We now combine the two twists and apply the transfer principle of Proposition <a href="#tr:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tr:transfer">11</a>. Retain $E,X$ and the finite side-projective bimodule $Y$ from Proposition <a href="#b:bimodule" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="b:bimodule">19</a>. For brevity, write $LX=L\otimes_E X$ for an $E$-bimodule $L$, and set $$W^a=\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_E(X,YX[a]).$$ Thus $W^a=k$ for $a=-3,0$ and is zero otherwise.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### The fiber of the two evaluated maps

<a id="t:fiber"></a>
For $i=1,2$, put $U_i=U_{H_i}$. Choose the maps $g_i:U_i\to Y$ supplied by Proposition <a href="#b:bimodule" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="b:bimodule">19</a>. Using the fixed identifications $U_iX\simeq X$, their evaluated stable classes are nonzero elements of the same one-dimensional space $W^0$. Rescale them so that both classes equal a fixed $w\ne0$. Choose a finite free $E$-bimodule $Q$ with a surjection $\pi:Q\to Y$, and define $F$ by $$\label{t:fiber}
 0\longrightarrow F\longrightarrow U_1\oplus U_2\oplus Q
 \xrightarrow{(g_1,g_2,\pi)}Y\longrightarrow0.$$ For example, the action gives such a surjection from $E^e\otimes_kY$. Since $Y$ is projective on each side, this sequence splits separately on the left and on the right. In particular, $F$ is finite and side-projective.

<a id="t:fiber-hom"></a>
Let $$\mathcal V^a=\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_E(X,FX[a]).$$ Evaluating <a href="#t:fiber" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="t:fiber">[t:fiber]</a> on $X$ preserves exactness, and $QX$ is $E$-projective. The stable long exact sequence is therefore $$\label{t:fiber-hom}
 (\mathcal H^{a-1})^2\longrightarrow W^{a-1}
 \longrightarrow\mathcal V^a
 \xrightarrow{p^a}(\mathcal H^a)^2\longrightarrow W^a.$$ In degree zero the last map is $(c,d)\mapsto(c+d)w$. It follows that $\mathcal V^0$ is a line with a unique element $v_0$ projecting to $(1,1)$.

<a id="t:delta"></a>
For a stable map $g:X\to X[a]$, let $F(g)$ denote its image under the exact functor $F\otimes_E-$, using the shift comparisons of Lemma <a href="#h:evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:evaluation">9</a>. Define $$\label{t:delta}
 \Delta^a:(\mathcal H^a)^2\longrightarrow\mathcal V^a,
 \qquad (g,h)\longmapsto F(g)v_0+v_0[a]h.$$

<a id="stmt-t-comparison-maps"></a>
<div id="t:comparison-maps" class="lemma">

**Lemma 26**. *The map $\Delta^0$ is surjective. For every $a>0$ and every $a\leq-3$, $\Delta^a$ is an isomorphism, and $\Delta^{-2}$ is injective.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-t-comparison-maps"><summary>Proof of stmt-t-comparison-maps</summary>

*Proof.* The projection $p^a$ in <a href="#t:fiber-hom" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="t:fiber-hom">[t:fiber-hom]</a> is an isomorphism for $a>0$. For $a\geq2$ this follows from the support of $W$; at $a=1$ it also uses the surjectivity of $(\mathcal H^0)^2\to W^0$. The same support argument applies for $a\leq-4$. At $a=-3$, both $W^{-4}$ and $\mathcal H^{-3}$ are zero, so $\mathcal V^{-3}=0$ and $p^{-3}$ is again an isomorphism. The remaining relevant low degrees can be read directly from <a href="#t:fiber-hom" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="t:fiber-hom">[t:fiber-hom]</a>: <a id="t:boundary-fiber"></a>$$\label{t:boundary-fiber}
 \mathcal V^{-2}=k,
 \qquad \mathcal V^0=kv_0,
 \qquad \mathcal V^1=0.$$

<a id="t:projected-delta"></a>
Naturality of the bimodule projections $F\to U_i$ gives $$\label{t:projected-delta}
 p^a\Delta^a(g,h)=
 \bigl(U_1(g)+h,\ U_2(g)+h\bigr).$$ Indeed each projection sends $v_0$ to the identity of $X$, and evaluation of a bimodule map commutes with the specified shift comparisons. By Proposition <a href="#e:twists" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="e:twists">18</a>, at $a=3m>0$ the map in <a href="#t:projected-delta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="t:projected-delta">[t:projected-delta]</a> is the scalar matrix $$\begin{pmatrix}H_1^{-m}&1\\H_2^{-m}&1\end{pmatrix}
 \otimes 1_{\mathcal P_m},$$ and at $a=-3m-1\leq-4$ it is $$\begin{pmatrix}H_1^{m+2}&1\\H_2^{m+2}&1\end{pmatrix}
 \otimes 1_{D\mathcal P_m}.$$ In both cases $m\geq1$. Algebraic independence of $H_1,H_2$ implies that each determinant is nonzero in $k$, for every such $m$. In all other degrees in the asserted ranges, the source and target are zero by Proposition <a href="#e:all-degrees" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="e:all-degrees">16</a> and the already proved isomorphism $p^a$.

Finally, $\Delta^0(c,d)=(c+d)v_0$ is surjective. Since $\mathcal H^{-2}=0$, the map $\Delta^{-2}:0\to\mathcal V^{-2}=k$ is injective. Its surjectivity is neither claimed nor needed. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### A cone over the triangular algebra

<a id="t:triangular-algebra"></a>
<a id="t:diagonal-hom"></a>
<a id="t:cross-hom"></a>
Form the finite-dimensional algebra $$\label{t:triangular-algebra}
 \Lambda=\begin{pmatrix}E&0\\F&E\end{pmatrix}.$$ A left $\Lambda$-module is a triple $(L,N,\alpha:F\otimes_E L\to N)$ of left $E$-modules and an $E$-linear structure map, with action $$\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\b&d\end{pmatrix}(x,y)
 =\bigl(ax,\alpha(b\otimes x)+dy\bigr).$$ The column constructions are standard in the theory of totally acyclic complexes over triangular matrix rings; see (Eshraghi et al. 2014, Lemma 2.2 and Proposition 2.3) <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[EshraghiEtAl2014v1](#ref-EshraghiEtAl2014v1)]. Here $F$ is projective on each side, so the required tensor exactness and orthogonality conditions hold. We retain the complete resolution to compute its self-Hom groups. Define the two column functors $$\mathsf D_0(L)=(L,FL,1),
 \qquad \mathsf D_1(L)=(0,L,0).$$ They are exact and preserve finite projectives: exactness uses right projectivity of $F$, and their values on $E$ are the two columns of the regular module $\Lambda$. The descriptions of module maps give $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_\Lambda(\mathsf D_i(L),\mathsf D_i(L'))
   &\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_E(L,L')\quad(i=0,1),\label{t:diagonal-hom}\\
 \mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_\Lambda(\mathsf D_0(L),\mathsf D_1(L'))&=0,
 \qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_\Lambda(\mathsf D_1(L),\mathsf D_0(L'))
   \simeq\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_E(L,FL').\label{t:cross-hom}
\end{aligned}$$ In the first diagonal slot, the map on the lower component is $F(g)$ when the map on the upper component is $g$.

Let $p_f=f\otimes f$. Use the embedding $i_X:X\hookrightarrow Ep_f$, $i_X(1)=\zeta$, established in Lemma <a href="#e:socle" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="e:socle">17</a>. Choose a complete resolution $P$ of $X$ over $E$ whose first negative term is $P_{-1}=Ep_f$, and whose degree-zero differential is $d_0=i_X\epsilon$, where $\epsilon:P_0\to X$ is the augmentation. Such a choice is possible because $E$ is symmetric. The complex $FP$ is exact with projective terms, and $c_0(FP)=FX$.

<a id="t:cone"></a>
Choose an ordinary map $\widetilde v:X\to FX$ representing $v_0$. Lemma <a href="#h:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:comparison">4</a> lifts it to a chain map $P\to FP$. By <a href="#t:cross-hom" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="t:cross-hom">[t:cross-hom]</a> this is a chain map $$V:\mathsf D_1(P)\longrightarrow\mathsf D_0(P).$$ Set $$\label{t:cone}
 P_Z=\mathop{\mathrm{Cone}}(V),\qquad Z=c_0P_Z.$$ Thus $$(P_Z)_j=\mathsf D_0(P_j)\oplus\mathsf D_1(P_{j-1}),$$ with cross differential $V_{j-1}$ from the second summand to the first summand in degree $j-1$.

<a id="stmt-t-finite-module"></a>
<div id="t:finite-module" class="lemma">

**Lemma 27**. *The complex $P_Z$ is totally acyclic. Its degree-zero cokernel $Z$ is finite-dimensional and nonprojective.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-t-finite-module"><summary>Proof of stmt-t-finite-module</summary>

*Proof.* Both column complexes have projective terms and are exact. Since $\Lambda=\mathsf D_0(E)\oplus\mathsf D_1(E)$, <a href="#t:diagonal-hom" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="t:diagonal-hom">[t:diagonal-hom]</a>–<a href="#t:cross-hom" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="t:cross-hom">[t:cross-hom]</a> identify their Hom complexes into $\Lambda$ with sums of $$\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_E(P,E),\qquad\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_E(P,F),\qquad0.$$ These are exact: $P$ is totally acyclic and $F$ is finite projective on the left. Hence both column complexes are totally acyclic, and so is their cone.

<a id="t:explicit-module"></a>
For an explicit finite description, $Z$ is the triple with upper component $X$ and lower component $$\label{t:explicit-module}
 N_Z=(FX\oplus Ep_f)
 \big/\{(\widetilde v(x),i_X(x)):x\in X\},$$ whose structure map $FX\to N_Z$ is induced by the first summand. To verify this formula, first quotient the degree-zero lower component by the image of the differential in $\mathsf D_0(P)$. This gives $FX$ in its first summand. The remaining relations arise from $P_0$ in the shifted second column. Its two components induce $\widetilde v\epsilon$ and $i_X\epsilon$, respectively, yielding <a href="#t:explicit-module" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="t:explicit-module">[t:explicit-module]</a>.

The upper-component functor sends the projective generators $\mathsf D_0(E)$ and $\mathsf D_1(E)$ to $E$ and $0$, and therefore sends every finite projective $\Lambda$-module to an $E$-projective. But $X$ is nonprojective, since its degree-three self-extension group is nonzero by Proposition <a href="#e:all-degrees" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="e:all-degrees">16</a>. Thus $Z$ is nonprojective. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### The full endomorphism complex

The complete resolution makes it possible to check both vanishing ranges with the same endomorphism calculation.

<a id="stmt-t-vanishing"></a>
<div id="t:vanishing" class="proposition">

**Proposition 28**. *The module and resolution in <a href="#t:cone" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="t:cone">[t:cone]</a> satisfy $$H^a\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_\Lambda(P_Z,Z)=0
 \qquad(a>0\ \text{or}\ a\leq-2).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-t-vanishing"><summary>Proof of stmt-t-vanishing</summary>

<a id="t:end-sequence"></a>
*Proof.* Write $A_0=\mathsf D_0(P)$ and $B_0=\mathsf D_1(P)$, so that the underlying graded complex of $P_Z$ is $A_0\oplus B_0[1]$. By <a href="#t:cross-hom" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="t:cross-hom">[t:cross-hom]</a>, the block $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_\Lambda^\bullet(A_0,B_0[1])$ is zero. Projection onto the two diagonal blocks therefore gives a short exact sequence of full Hom complexes $$\label{t:end-sequence}
\begin{split}
 0\longrightarrow\mathcal C^\bullet
 \longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{End}}_\Lambda^\bullet(P_Z)
 \longrightarrow
 \mathop{\mathrm{End}}_\Lambda^\bullet(A_0)\oplus
 \mathop{\mathrm{End}}_\Lambda^\bullet(B_0[1])
 \longrightarrow0,\\[-2pt]
 \mathcal C^\bullet=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_\Lambda^\bullet(B_0[1],A_0).
\end{split}$$ The underlying degreewise Hom spaces are products as in Lemma <a href="#h:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:comparison">4</a>; the block decomposition has only four blocks. In particular, it does not replace any product by a direct sum.

The diagonal cohomology is $(\mathcal H^a)^2$. To fix the cross-block index explicitly, in degree $a$ we have $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal C^a
 &=\prod_j\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_E(P_{j-1},FP_{j-a})\\
 &=\prod_i\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_E(P_i,FP_{i-(a-1)})
   =\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_E^{a-1}(P,FP).
\end{aligned}$$ The differentials agree under this reindexing in characteristic two. Lemma <a href="#h:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:comparison">4</a> consequently gives $H^a\mathcal C^\bullet=\mathcal V^{a-1}$.

<a id="t:end-cohomology"></a>
The connecting map in <a href="#t:end-sequence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="t:end-sequence">[t:end-sequence]</a>, from diagonal cohomology in degree $a$ to $H^{a+1}\mathcal C^\bullet=\mathcal V^a$, is precisely $\Delta^a$. Indeed, lift two diagonal cocycles to the diagonal blocks of an endomorphism of the cone and take its cochain differential. The cross component is the sum of its two compositions with $V$. The first diagonal acts downstairs by $F$ of the corresponding map, while the second acts by precomposition. Under the composition-compatible comparison in Lemma <a href="#h:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:comparison">4</a>, this gives $F(g)v_0+v_0[a]h$, as in <a href="#t:delta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="t:delta">[t:delta]</a>. Thus the long exact sequence gives $$\label{t:end-cohomology}
 0\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{coker}}\Delta^{a-1}
 \longrightarrow H^a\mathop{\mathrm{End}}_\Lambda^\bullet(P_Z)
 \longrightarrow\ker\Delta^a\longrightarrow0.$$

For $a>1$ or $a\leq-3$, both outer terms vanish by Lemma <a href="#t:comparison-maps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="t:comparison-maps">26</a>. At $a=1$, they vanish by surjectivity of $\Delta^0$ and injectivity of $\Delta^1$. At $a=-2$, they vanish by the isomorphism $\Delta^{-3}$ and injectivity of $\Delta^{-2}$. Finally, apply Lemma <a href="#h:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:comparison">4</a> to the totally acyclic complex $P_Z$ to identify this cohomology with $H^a\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_\Lambda(P_Z,Z)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-t-neighboring-degrees"></a>
<div id="t:neighboring-degrees" class="remark">

*Remark 29*. The boundary degrees can also be located completely. Equation <a href="#t:fiber-hom" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="t:fiber-hom">[t:fiber-hom]</a> gives $\mathcal V^{-1}=(\mathcal H^{-1})^2=k^2$. By Proposition <a href="#e:twists" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="e:twists">18</a>, the two rows of $\Delta^{-1}$ are $(H_i^2,1)$, so this map is invertible. Since $\Delta^{-2}:0\to k$ and $\ker\Delta^0=k$, <a href="#t:end-cohomology" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="t:end-cohomology">[t:end-cohomology]</a> yields $$H^a\mathop{\mathrm{End}}_\Lambda^\bullet(P_Z)=
 \begin{cases}
 k,&a=-1,0,\\
 0,&a\notin\{-1,0\}.
 \end{cases}$$ In particular the nonzero cokernel of $\Delta^{-2}$ contributes in degree $-1$, while degree $-2$ vanishes as required by Proposition <a href="#tr:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tr:transfer">11</a>.

</div>

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### Completion of the proof

<details id="proof-stmt-t-neighboring-degrees"><summary>Proof of stmt-t-neighboring-degrees</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Fix the finite bimodule maps in <a href="#t:fiber" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="t:fiber">[t:fiber]</a>, the complete resolution $P$, and the lifted map used in <a href="#t:cone" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="t:cone">[t:cone]</a>. These choices exist over $k$ by the preceding propositions. The algebra $\Lambda$ in <a href="#t:triangular-algebra" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="t:triangular-algebra">[t:triangular-algebra]</a> and its module $Z$ in <a href="#t:explicit-module" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="t:explicit-module">[t:explicit-module]</a> are finite-dimensional. By Lemma <a href="#t:finite-module" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="t:finite-module">27</a>, $Z$ is nonprojective and has the totally acyclic resolution $P_Z$. Proposition <a href="#t:vanishing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="t:vanishing">28</a> supplies both hypotheses on its complete self-Hom groups in Proposition <a href="#tr:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tr:transfer">11</a>. Apply that proposition with $R=\Lambda$ to obtain $$A=\Lambda\ltimes D\Lambda,
 \qquad M=A\otimes_\Lambda Z.$$ It proves that $A$ is finite-dimensional and symmetric, that $M$ is finite-dimensional and nonprojective, and that $\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_A^i(M,M)=0$ for every $i>0$. These are all the assertions of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:consequences"></a>
### The endomorphism algebra and homological consequences

The symmetric pair constructed in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> has a self-orthogonal generator $A\oplus M$. The classical relation between such generators and dominant dimension turns the module counterexample into an algebra whose entire regular injective resolution is projective. We first isolate this step, then extract the modules in Corollaries <a href="#cor:homological" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:homological">2</a> and <a href="#cor:finitistic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:finitistic">3</a>.

<a id="stmt-prop-endomorphism"></a>
<div id="prop:endomorphism" class="proposition">

**Proposition 30**. *Let $B$ be a finite-dimensional symmetric algebra over a field and let $N$ be a finite-dimensional nonprojective left $B$-module satisfying $\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_B^i(N,N)=0$ for all $i>0$. Put $$G=B\oplus N,\qquad \Gamma=\mathop{\mathrm{End}}_B(G)^{\mathrm{op}},$$ where endomorphisms compose in the ordinary order. Then $$\mathop{\mathrm{domdim}}({}_\Gamma\Gamma)=\infty,
 \qquad \mathop{\mathrm{id}}_\Gamma\Gamma=\infty.$$ In particular, $\Gamma$ is not self-injective.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-endomorphism"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-endomorphism</summary>

*Proof.* The module $G$ is a generator-cogenerator: it contains the regular module, and symmetry identifies $B$ with its dual, an injective cogenerator. Moreover $$\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_B^i(G,G)=0\qquad(i>0).$$ The $N,N$ summand vanishes by hypothesis. The summands with first argument $B$ vanish by projectivity, and the remaining summand with second argument $B$ vanishes by injectivity.

Mueller’s criterion (Mueller 1968, Lemma 3) <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[Mueller1968](#ref-Mueller1968)] says that, for a generator-cogenerator $G$, vanishing of these groups for $1\leq i\leq n$ is equivalent to dominant dimension at least $n+2$ for its endomorphism algebra. His Theorem 4 identifies the left and right dominant dimensions, so the opposite convention in the definition of $\Gamma$ makes no difference to this assertion. Applying the criterion for every $n$ proves $\mathop{\mathrm{domdim}}({}_\Gamma\Gamma)=\infty$.

We verify non-self-injectivity directly. The functor $$H=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_B(G,-):\mathop{\mathrm{add}}G\longrightarrow \Gamma\text{-}\mathrm{proj}$$ is fully faithful and sends $G$ to the left regular module $\Gamma$. Explicitly, if $e\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}_B(G)$ and $h:G\to V$, then $e^{\mathrm{op}}h=h\circ e$; full faithfulness follows first on finite sums of $G$ and then on their summands. Suppose that the left regular module $\Gamma$ were injective. Its direct summand $H(N)$ would be injective as well. Choose a finite injective envelope $N\hookrightarrow J$ over $B$. Since $B$ is symmetric, $J\in\mathop{\mathrm{add}}B\subseteq\mathop{\mathrm{add}}G$. Left exactness of $H$ gives a monomorphism $H(N)\hookrightarrow H(J)$, which splits by injectivity of $H(N)$. Full faithfulness gives a retraction $J\to N$ of the original inclusion. Thus $N$ is injective, hence projective over the symmetric algebra $B$, a contradiction.

Finally, every term in the minimal injective resolution of $\Gamma$ is projective by infinite dominant dimension. If its injective dimension were finite, this resolution would end in a finite exact sequence $$0\longrightarrow\Gamma\longrightarrow I^0\longrightarrow\cdots
 \longrightarrow I^d\longrightarrow0$$ of projective-injective terms. For $d>0$, the last surjection splits because $I^d$ is projective. Its kernel is then projective, and repeating backwards shows that the first inclusion splits. The case $d=0$ is immediate. In either case $\Gamma$ is a summand of $I^0$ and is injective, contrary to the preceding paragraph. Hence $\mathop{\mathrm{id}}_\Gamma\Gamma=\infty$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-endomorphism-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-endomorphism</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#cor:homological" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:homological">2</a>.* Fix a field extension $K/k$. The symmetric structure survives extension of scalars: since $A$ is finite-dimensional, the natural bimodule identification $$K\otimes_k DA\simeq D_K(K\otimes_k A),
 \qquad D_K=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_K(-,K),$$ turns $A\simeq DA$ into a symmetrizing isomorphism for $A_K$.

<a id="c:basechange"></a>
We also record why the module hypotheses survive. For finite $A$-modules $U,V$, resolve $U$ by finite projective modules. Finite projectivity gives, in every degree, $$K\otimes_k\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_A(P_i,V)
 \simeq
 \mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{A_K}(K\otimes_kP_i,K\otimes_kV).$$ Flatness commutes with kernels and images, so $$\label{c:basechange}
 K\otimes_k\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_A^i(U,V)
 \simeq \mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_{A_K}^i(K\otimes_kU,K\otimes_kV)
 \qquad(i\geq0).$$ Thus $M_K$ is self-orthogonal. Choose a finite projective surjection $P\twoheadrightarrow M$ with kernel $L$. Its extension class in $\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_A^1(M,L)$ is nonzero, since $M$ is not projective. It remains nonzero under <a href="#c:basechange" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="c:basechange">[c:basechange]</a>, because extension of fields is faithful. The extended surjection therefore does not split, and $M_K$ is nonprojective. The degree-zero case of <a href="#c:basechange" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="c:basechange">[c:basechange]</a> is compatible with composition and also identifies $$\Gamma_K\simeq K\otimes_k\mathop{\mathrm{End}}_A(A\oplus M)^{\mathrm{op}}.$$ These are the same finite Ext and nonprojectivity base-change arguments used in the companion (OpenAI 2026b, Proposition 9.2) <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[OpenAIAR2026](#ref-OpenAIAR2026)].

Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:endomorphism" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:endomorphism">30</a> to $A_K,M_K$, and abbreviate $\Gamma_K$ to $\Gamma$. Write $$0\longrightarrow\Gamma\longrightarrow I^0
 \longrightarrow I^1\longrightarrow\cdots$$ for its minimal injective resolution. Every $I^j$ is projective-injective and $\mathop{\mathrm{id}}_\Gamma\Gamma=\infty$. This proves the first assertion and refutes the classical Nakayama conjecture. It also refutes the Auslander–Gorenstein conjecture: $\mathop{\mathrm{pd}}_\Gamma I^j=0\leq j$ for every $j\geq0$, whereas the left self-injective dimension is already infinite.

There is a nonprojective indecomposable injective left $\Gamma$-module $J$. Indeed, finite-dimensional algebras have the same finite number of isomorphism classes of indecomposable projectives and indecomposable injectives, both indexed by the simple modules. If every indecomposable injective were projective, these classes would exhaust the indecomposable projectives, making the regular module injective. This contradicts Proposition <a href="#prop:endomorphism" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:endomorphism">30</a>. Since every $I^j$ is projective, none contains $J$ as a direct summand, giving the omitted-injective failure of the generalized Nakayama conjecture.

Let $S=\mathop{\mathrm{soc}}J$. The socle of an indecomposable injective over a finite-dimensional algebra is simple, and $J$ is its injective envelope. If there were a nonzero map $S\to I^j$, it would be injective. Injectivity of $I^j$ would extend it to a map $J\to I^j$. The extension is injective because $S$ is essential in $J$, and it splits because $J$ is injective. This would make $J$ a summand of $I^j$, a contradiction. Hence $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_\Gamma(S,I^j)=0$ for every $j\geq0$. Applying $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_\Gamma(S,-)$ to the resolution gives $$\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_\Gamma^i(S,\Gamma)=0\qquad(i\geq0).$$ The nonzero module $S$ therefore refutes the strong Nakayama conjecture and also gives the simple-Ext formulation of generalized Nakayama failure. In particular, the degree-zero vanishing is part of the conclusion, not only the positive-degree vanishings.

Finally let $W$ be the direct sum of one representative from each isomorphism class of indecomposable projective-injective left $\Gamma$-modules. There are finitely many such classes, so $W$ is finite, projective, and injective. In particular $\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_\Gamma^i(W,W)=0$ for $i>0$, and every $I^j$ belongs to $\mathop{\mathrm{add}}W$. The displayed injective resolution is therefore an infinite $\mathop{\mathrm{add}}W$-coresolution of $\Gamma$. Applying $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_\Gamma(-,W)$ preserves its exactness because $W$ is injective. Thus $W$ is Wakamatsu tilting and has projective dimension zero.

If $W$ were tilting, there would be a finite exact coresolution $$0\longrightarrow\Gamma\longrightarrow W^0\longrightarrow\cdots
 \longrightarrow W^r\longrightarrow0,\qquad W^j\in\mathop{\mathrm{add}}W.$$ Every $W^j$ is projective-injective. The backward splitting argument in Proposition <a href="#prop:endomorphism" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:endomorphism">30</a> would make $\Gamma$ injective, a contradiction. Hence $W$ is not tilting, which refutes the Wakamatsu tilting conjecture. ◻

</details>

The proof works separately over each extension $K/k$. It does not require a minimal injective resolution to remain minimal under scalar extension, or an indecomposable injective or simple module to remain indecomposable or simple. The construction of $\Gamma_K$ is compatible with scalar extension, and the required $J,S,W$ are chosen over $K$.

The successive cokernels of the same injective resolution give the finitistic-dimension consequence.

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-endomorphism-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-endomorphism</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#cor:finitistic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:finitistic">3</a>.* Fix a field extension $K/k$ and abbreviate $\Gamma_K$ to $\Gamma$. Use the minimal injective resolution of the left regular module over this field, $$0\longrightarrow {}_\Gamma\Gamma\longrightarrow I^0
 \longrightarrow I^1\longrightarrow\cdots.$$ Every $I^n$ is finite-dimensional and projective-injective by Corollary <a href="#cor:homological" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:homological">2</a>. Set $C_0={}_\Gamma\Gamma$ and $C_n=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}(I^{n-1}\to I^n)$ for $n\geq1$. Exactness gives $$0\longrightarrow C_n\longrightarrow I^n\longrightarrow C_{n+1}
 \longrightarrow0\qquad(n\geq0).$$ Thus the $C_{n+1}$ are successive cokernels, and all $C_n$ are finite.

Since $C_0$ and $I^0$ are projective, the first sequence gives $\mathop{\mathrm{pd}}_\Gamma C_1\leq1$. If $C_1$ were projective, its surjection from $I^0$ would split, making $\Gamma$ a direct summand of the injective module $I^0$. This would make $\Gamma$ injective, contrary to $\mathop{\mathrm{id}}_\Gamma\Gamma=\infty$. Hence $\mathop{\mathrm{pd}}_\Gamma C_1=1$.

Suppose $n\geq1$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{pd}}_\Gamma C_n=n$. Splicing a length-$n$ projective resolution of $C_n$ with the displayed short exact sequence gives $\mathop{\mathrm{pd}}_\Gamma C_{n+1}\leq n+1$. For every left module $V$ and $j\geq1$, projectivity of $I^n$ and the long exact Ext sequence give $$\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_\Gamma^j(C_n,V)\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_\Gamma^{j+1}(C_{n+1},V).$$ There is a left module $V$ with $\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_\Gamma^n(C_n,V)\neq0$, since otherwise the Ext criterion for projective dimension would give $\mathop{\mathrm{pd}}_\Gamma C_n\leq n-1$. Thus $\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_\Gamma^{n+1}(C_{n+1},V)\neq0$, proving the reverse bound and $\mathop{\mathrm{pd}}_\Gamma C_{n+1}=n+1$. Induction gives the asserted exact dimensions. The finite left modules $C_n$ therefore make the little left finitistic dimension infinite. The big left finitistic dimension is at least the little one, so it is infinite as well. ◻

</details>

The remaining applications use the established failures of the homological conjectures together with results on tilting modules and stratifying ideals.

A finite left module $P$ over a finite-dimensional algebra $\Lambda$ is *almost complete tilting* if it is a direct summand of a tilting module and has one fewer isomorphism class of indecomposable summands than there are isomorphism classes of simple left $\Lambda$-modules. Here tilting has the ordinary finite-projective-dimension meaning recalled in Section <a href="#sec:introduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:introduction">1</a>. An *indecomposable complement* is a finite indecomposable module $Y$ such that $P\oplus Y$ is tilting.

<a id="stmt-cor-tilting-complements"></a>
<div id="cor:tilting-complements" class="corollary">

**Corollary 31** (Infinitely many tilting complements). *For every field extension $K/k$, let $\Gamma_K$ be the algebra in Corollary <a href="#cor:homological" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:homological">2</a>. There is a finite-dimensional projective almost complete tilting left $\Gamma_K^{\mathrm{op}}$-module $P_K$ with infinitely many pairwise nonisomorphic indecomposable complements.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-tilting-complements"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-tilting-complements</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $K$ and abbreviate $\Gamma_K$ to $\Gamma$. By Corollary <a href="#cor:homological" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:homological">2</a>, $\Gamma$ fails the generalized Nakayama conjecture. Buan–Solberg (Buan and Solberg 1998, Theorems 3.1 and 3.4) <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[BuanSolberg1998](#ref-BuanSolberg1998)] therefore give an injective almost complete cotilting left $\Gamma$-module $U$ with infinitely many pairwise nonisomorphic indecomposable complements $X$. The exact duality $D_K=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_K(-,K)$ sends finite left $\Gamma$-modules to finite left $\Gamma^{\mathrm{op}}$-modules. It sends finite injective dimension to finite projective dimension, preserves self-orthogonality, and dualizes the finite cotilting resolution of the injective cogenerator into a finite tilting coresolution of the regular module. Thus $P_K=D_KU$ is projective and almost complete tilting, and $D_KX$ are its complements. Duality preserves indecomposability and distinct isomorphism classes, so there are infinitely many such complements. The witnesses are chosen separately over each extension $K/k$. ◻

</details>

The final application concerns decompositions of derived categories. For an idempotent $e$ in a finite-dimensional algebra $R$, the ideal $ReR$ is *stratifying* if multiplication induces an isomorphism $$Re\otimes_{eRe}eR\xrightarrow{\sim}ReR$$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}_i^{eRe}(Re,eR)=0$ for every $i>0$. It then induces the standard recollement relating the unbounded derived categories of $R/ReR$, $R$, and $eRe$; see (Chen, Fang, and Xi 2024, Introduction) <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[ChenFangXi2024](#ref-ChenFangXi2024)]. An *idempotent stratification of length $n$*, in the convention of (Chen, Fang, and Xi 2024, Definition 4.7) <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[ChenFangXi2024](#ref-ChenFangXi2024)], consists of nonzero mutually orthogonal idempotents $e_0,\ldots,e_n$ with sum $1$ such that, on putting $f_i=e_0+\cdots+e_i$, the ideal generated by $f_i$ in $f_{i+1}Rf_{i+1}$ is stratifying and $e_{i+1}\notin Rf_iR$ for $0\leq i<n$. Thus its length counts the successive nontrivial recollements obtained by passing through these corner algebras.

<a id="stmt-cor-stratifying"></a>
<div id="cor:stratifying" class="corollary">

**Corollary 32** (Idempotent stratifications). *For every field extension $K/k$ and every integer $L\geq1$, there is an auxiliary finite-dimensional indecomposable symmetric $K$-algebra $T_L$ admitting an idempotent stratification of length at least $L$ in the sense of Chen, Fang and Xi. In particular, $T_L$ has a nonzero proper stratifying ideal, and $\mathcal D(T_L\text{-}\mathrm{Mod})$ admits a chain of at least $L$ nontrivial standard recollements through successive corner algebras. Here $\mathrm{Mod}$ denotes all left modules and $\mathcal D$ is the unbounded derived category.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-stratifying"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-stratifying</summary>

*Proof.* The base-change argument above gives a symmetric $K$-algebra $A_K$ and a nonprojective self-orthogonal module $M_K$. Choose a nonprojective indecomposable summand $N$ in one block $B$ of $A_K$. The block $B$ is indecomposable and symmetric, and $\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_B^i(N,N)=0$ for every $i>0$, since these groups are direct summands of $\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_{A_K}^i(M_K,M_K)$.

Chen, Fang and Xi (Chen, Fang, and Xi 2024, proof of Theorem 5.13) <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[ChenFangXi2024](#ref-ChenFangXi2024)] construct from such a pair, over any field, reduced mirror-reflective algebras $T_n$ that are indecomposable and symmetric. Their Corollary 5.12(1), with Definition 4.7, gives an idempotent stratification of length at least $n$. Theorem 1.2(2) supplies the stated successive nontrivial standard recollements of unbounded derived categories. Taking $n=L$ proves the claim. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "Constructions and implications in the proof. The complete self-Hom vanishing for $(Λ,Z)$ supplies the hypotheses for transfer to $(A,M)$. Arrows indicate proof stages, not algebra maps.",
      "figure_id": "fig:proof-architecture",
      "pdf_page": 4,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:proof-architecture",
          "exact_quote": "Constructions and implications in the proof. The complete\nself-Hom vanishing for $(\\Lambda,Z)$ supplies the hypotheses for\ntransfer to $(A,M)$. Arrows indicate proof stages, not algebra maps.",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth,\n box/.style={draw,rounded corners=2pt,align=center,text width=4.8cm,\n             inner sep=6pt,font=\\small},\n lab/.style={font=\\footnotesize,align=center}]\n\\node[box] (ex) at (0,3.7)\n {$E=T\\otimes T$, $X=s\\otimes s$\\\\$\\Ext_E^*(X,X)=k[\\tau_1,\\tau_2]$};\n\\node[box] (y) at (6.6,3.7)\n {$Y$ and $g_H:U_H\\to Y$\\\\$W^a=0$ for $a\\notin\\{-3,0\\}$};\n\\node[box] (fv) at (6.6,1)\n {$F$ and $v:X\\to F\\otimes_E X$};\n\\node[box] (lz) at (0,1)\n {$(\\Lambda,Z)$ and $P_Z$\\\\$H^a\\Hom_\\Lambda(P_Z,Z)=0$\\\\for $a>0$ or $a\\leq-2$};\n\\node[box] (am) at (0,-1.6)\n {$A=\\Lambda\\ltimes D\\Lambda$\\\\$M=A\\otimes_\\Lambda Z$\\\\symmetric counterexample};\n\\draw[->] (ex)--node[above=9mm,lab]{derived induction\\\\and trace obstruction}(y);\n\\draw[->] (y)--node[right,lab]{two twists\\\\and finite fiber}(fv);\n\\draw[->] (fv)--node[above=9mm,lab]{triangular cone}(lz);\n\\draw[->] (lz)--node[right,lab]{trivial extension\\\\and induction}(am);\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A flowchart diagram showing five rectangular nodes connected by arrows:\n- Top-left node contains:\n  $E = T \\otimes T, \\ X = s \\otimes s$\n  $\\operatorname{Ext}^*_E(X, X) = k[\\tau_1, \\tau_2]$\n- Top-right node contains:\n  $Y \\text{ and } g_H : U_H \\to Y$\n  $W^a = 0 \\text{ for } a \\notin \\{-3, 0\\}$\n- Middle-right node contains:\n  $F \\text{ and } v : X \\to F \\otimes_E X$\n- Middle-left node contains:\n  $(\\Lambda, Z) \\text{ and } P_Z$\n  $H^a \\operatorname{Hom}_\\Lambda(P_Z, Z) = 0$\n  $\\text{for } a > 0 \\text{ or } a \\le -2$\n- Bottom node contains:\n  $A = \\Lambda \\ltimes D\\Lambda$\n  $M = A \\otimes_\\Lambda Z$\n  symmetric counterexample\nArrows between nodes:\n- Rightward horizontal arrow from top-left node to top-right node, labeled above with \"derived induction and trace obstruction\".\n- Downward vertical arrow from top-right node to middle-right node, labeled to the right with \"two twists and finite fiber\".\n- Leftward horizontal arrow from middle-right node to middle-left node, labeled above with \"triangular cone\".\n- Downward vertical arrow from middle-left node to bottom node, labeled with \"trivial extension and induction\"."
    }
